\subsection{Retrieval and memory consistency}

\begin{itemize}

\item The source system revision is authoritative. Index entries are disposable projections and must carry source revision, ACL pointer, ingestion time, and deletion state.

\item Search performs tenant/source pruning, exact ACL filtering, hybrid lexical/vector scoring, reranking, freshness filtering, and diversity/deduplication before context selection.

\item A memory write is a governed business operation: validate type, scope, consent, provenance, contradiction, retention, and sensitivity. Derived guesses default to run-scoped working memory, not long-term memory.

\item Correcting or deleting a source creates lineage events that invalidate affected memories, summaries, embeddings, model caches, and evidence packages according to retention/legal-hold policy.

\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scalability and token economics}

\begin{itemize}

\item Partition conversations and memories by tenant + subject/team; partition knowledge by tenant + source + region. Use routing metadata so a query touches only eligible partitions.

\item Store content in object storage and metadata in relational/search stores. Context APIs exchange references; only the assembler materializes bytes for the authorized model call.

\item Run ingestion as an asynchronous CDC/event pipeline with separate extract, classify, ACL, chunk, embed, and index stages. Track independent cursors so failed embedding does not block ACL deletion.

\item Cache retrieval candidates by tenant, source revision, normalized query, policy scope, and embedding/reranker version; re-run exact authorization on cache hit.

\item Allocate a fixed context budget before retrieval. Use maximum marginal relevance/source diversity, per-source caps, and evidence-quality thresholds rather than filling the entire window.

\item Compact long conversations asynchronously and incrementally. Keep recent turns plus source-linked summaries; rebuild summaries when covered inputs change.

\item Enforce deletion SLOs across primary metadata, vector/search indexes, caches, and derived summaries. Tombstones must outlive the longest replica/cache lag to prevent resurrection.

\end{itemize}

\begin{bookcost}[Performance and cost]
Retrieve less, and cite what you retrieved. Context is the dominant cost in most agentic systems and the dominant cause of distraction. Record a context manifest identifier rather than the context body, and measure answer quality against context size — most systems are past the point where more retrieval helps.
\end{bookcost}

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endhead
Privacy lifecycle & Context and memory need purpose limitation, consent or other legal basis where applicable, data-subject request handling, deletion propagation, legal-hold precedence, residency, provider retention controls, and proof that derived summaries/indexes were handled too. & R13; R39 \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

The discussion above draws on R13; R39. Appendix K lists the complete sources and records their authority, version, and scope.\par

\section{How Memory works in practice}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Engineering Problem]
The system should reuse useful information across turns or runs without confusing stale summaries with canonical truth or retaining data indefinitely.
\end{bookcallout}

\subsection{How the pattern works}

Working memory is run-scoped; long-term memory is a governed write with subject, scope, provenance, confidence, policy basis, TTL, correction, deletion, and retrieval authorization.\par

\begin{enumerate}

\item Identify a candidate reusable fact.

\item Validate evidence, subject, purpose, consent or policy basis, and sensitivity.

\item Write a versioned memory record with TTL and provenance.

\item Retrieve only after authorization and trust labeling.

\item Correct, supersede, expire, or delete through propagation workflows.

\end{enumerate}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Do not use when} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Do not use when} \\
\endhead
Use for stable preferences, repeated domain facts, or learned operational hints with clear value and governance. & Do not persist transient chain-of-thought, secrets, unverified hypotheses, protected data without basis, or facts already authoritative elsewhere. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Where it helps—and what it costs}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Choice} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Consequence} \\
Continuity & Creates privacy, staleness, correction, and deletion obligations. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Reduced context cost & May lose nuance or introduce summary drift. \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Where it can fail—and how to contain it}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Control} \\
Stale memory overrides state & Canonical state precedence and freshness checks. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Cross-tenant leakage & Tenant-partitioned keys and authorization before retrieval. \\
Deletion gaps & Lineage graph, tombstones, cache invalidation, and rebuild. \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{What you need to see in production}

\begin{itemize}

\item memory ID and version

\item source provenance

\item scope and tenant

\item policy basis

\item confidence and TTL

\item retrieval and deletion events

\end{itemize}

\section{A sensible place to start}

\subsection{Auditable context projection}

\BookSnake · implementation excerpt\par

\needspace{240.8pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
from __future__ import annotations

from datetime import datetime
from typing import Literal
from pydantic import BaseModel


class ContextSegmentRef(BaseModel):
    artifact_id: str
    source_revision: str
    sha256: str
    authorization_decision_id: str
    trust_role: Literal["instruction", "user_intent", "evidence"]
    token_count: int


class ContextManifest(BaseModel):
    context_id: str
    run_id: str
    release_id: str
    policy_version: str
    segments: tuple[ContextSegmentRef, ...]
    omitted_candidate_ids: tuple[str, ...]
    created_at: datetime
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Cross-boundary artifact adoption policy}

\BookSnake · implementation excerpt\par

\needspace{388.0pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
from __future__ import annotations

from datetime import datetime
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field


class ArtifactDescriptor(BaseModel):
    artifact_id: str
    tenant_id: str
    data_classes: frozenset[str]
    region: str
    allowed_purposes: frozenset[str]
    provenance_digest: str
    expires_at: datetime
    legal_hold: bool = False


class AdoptionPolicy(BaseModel):
    tenant_id: str
    allowed_data_classes: frozenset[str]
    allowed_regions: frozenset[str]
    purpose: str
    trusted_provenance_digests: frozenset[str]


def authorize_artifact_adoption(
    artifact: ArtifactDescriptor, policy: AdoptionPolicy, *, now: datetime
) -> None:
    if artifact.tenant_id != policy.tenant_id:
        raise PermissionError("cross-tenant artifact adoption denied")
    if not artifact.data_classes <= policy.allowed_data_classes:
        raise PermissionError("artifact classification exceeds policy")
    if artifact.region not in policy.allowed_regions:
        raise PermissionError("artifact region is not allowed")
    if policy.purpose not in artifact.allowed_purposes:
        raise PermissionError("artifact purpose is not allowed")
    if artifact.provenance_digest not in policy.trusted_provenance_digests:
        raise PermissionError("artifact provenance is not trusted")
    if now > artifact.expires_at and not artifact.legal_hold:
        raise PermissionError("artifact expired")
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Reconstruct one ContextManifest without relying on logged plaintext.

\item Compare canonical state with summaries and working memory.

\item Trace a corrected or deleted fact through indexes, cache, memory, and artifacts.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Conversation, workflow state, working memory, long-term memory, knowledge, artifacts, telemetry, and audit are separate domains.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Authorization occurs before content materialization.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Context records ordered references, hashes, trust labels, decisions, versions, redactions, and token allocation.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Memory creation is a governed write with scope, provenance, confidence, lawful basis, TTL, correction, and deletion.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Correction and deletion propagate through indexes, caches, summaries, memories, contexts, and artifacts.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Design a token budget across instructions, state, evidence, memory, and reserve.

\item Define the lifecycle of a reusable mortgage evidence-review preference memory.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
Why call the prompt a projection rather than state? & It is a temporary, token-bounded view assembled from authoritative stores for one model call; it can be rebuilt, audited by reference, and discarded. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How do conversation, knowledge, working memory, and long-term memory differ? & Interaction history; source-backed enterprise facts; run-scoped hypotheses; and approved reusable facts—each with different ownership, retention, authority, and deletion. \\
What problem does a ContextManifest solve? & Reproducibility and diagnosis through ordered source, version, and hash references, authorization decisions, trust labels, redactions, and budget without logging all plaintext. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How should retrieved instructions be handled? & As untrusted data with provenance and taint; they cannot alter system instructions, tool eligibility, policy, credentials, or destination grants. \\
How do you delete a fact from an agentic system? & Tombstone and revoke the source, remove derived indexes, invalidate caches, rebuild summaries and contexts, clean governed memory and affected artifacts, and preserve permitted audit evidence. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Context assembly completes the knowledge Part. Chapter 7 turns from what agents know to how teams build with it, defining the reusable developer platform and SDK.\par

\BookPartPage{III}{THE DEVELOPER PLATFORM}{Good architecture is not enough if every delivery team has to reconstruct it from scratch. This Part turns the reference design into a paved road: reusable contracts, adapters, Skills, domain packs, and a lifecycle that carries them from a developer laptop to production. The platform stays opinionated about safety and evidence while leaving domain teams room to build something useful.}

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-07-build-a-paved-road-for-agent-teams.png}

\BookChapter{07}{Build a Paved Road for Agent Teams}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} The portability layer is informed by current public agent abstractions from OpenAI, Microsoft, and Google; capability parity is not assumed and must be proven by adapter conformance tests. \textbf{\BookCitation{R29}\BookCitation{R31}\BookCitation{R32}}

\end{bookquote}

\section{Why teams should not each rebuild the safety layer}

Without a platform, every team rebuilds the same dangerous plumbing and every fix arrives at a different speed. This chapter describes a paved road made of contracts, adapters, and delivery practices. The goal is not to force every application into one monolith; it is to make the safe path easier to reuse than the clever shortcut.\par

\section{When the need becomes obvious}

The need for a paved road becomes obvious after the second or third team ships. One team retries inside its SDK wrapper, another records only transcripts, and a third gives tools a broad service credential. Each local choice looks reasonable, but the organization now has three incompatible meanings of failure, audit, and authorization. A platform earns its place by making those cross-cutting decisions once and leaving product teams free to build differentiated domain behavior.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Platform services own contracts and evidence; applications own domain behavior & application code to platform-owned control planes & template · adapter · release artifact & one application release & AgentSpec · RunContext · ToolManifest · ReleaseManifest \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{What teams should be able to rely on}

The developer platform is a reusable paved road that lets teams build differentiated agents without repeatedly rebuilding identity, policy, tool safety, tracing, evaluation, durable execution, and delivery infrastructure. Applications use platform-owned contracts and replaceable adapters.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/developer-platform.pdf}{Figure 7-1 — Agentic developer platform and SDK capability stack}{developer-platform}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Capability} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Reusable platform responsibility} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Application-specific responsibility} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Capability} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Reusable platform responsibility} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Application-specific responsibility} \\
\endhead
Agent Runtime & Run context, budgets, typed outputs, interruption, cancellation, adapter & Domain instructions, acceptance, specialist roles \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Workflow/Orchestration & Durable state, timers, retries, joins, compensation, replay & Domain process graph and deadlines \\
Tool SDK & Schema, identity context, idempotency, effect protocol, mocks, telemetry & Domain operation and compensation semantics \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
MCP Integration & Server registry, trust, gateway, discovery cache, compatibility tests & Approved servers/tools and domain interpretation \\
Agent-to-Agent Communication & Task/artifact envelopes, delegation, identity, deadline, cancellation & Collaboration topology and child acceptance \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Context/RAG & Connectors, ACL filtering, snapshot/provenance, ranking hooks, budgets & Sources, retrieval policy, evidence requirements \\
Memory & Eligibility, scope, TTL, provenance, correction/deletion, encryption & Purpose, schema, confidence, business ownership \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Skills & Manifest, versioning, dependencies, signing, registry, tests & Capability logic, knowledge, examples, acceptance suite \\
Identity \& Permissions & Subject/workload identity, policy, short-lived credentials & Declared permissions and resource scopes \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Guardrails & Injection, input/output safety, DLP, action policy & Domain risk rules, safe responses, escalation \\
LLM Gateway & Provider adapters, routing, budgets, filtering, cache, fallback, telemetry & Quality/cost/latency policy and needed capabilities \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Evaluation & Dataset/evaluator registry, experiments, regression gates & Domain truth, rubrics, thresholds, reviewers \\
Observability/Tracing & Unified run graph, schemas, sampling, redaction, correlation & Domain events and outcome linkage \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Durable Execution & Checkpoints, leases, deduplication, recovery, reconciliation & Domain state machine and compensation choices \\
Human-in-the-Loop & Proposal, preview, grants, queues, SLAs, recourse & Reviewer policy, domain evidence, decision rights \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Deployment \& Versioning & Packaging, manifests, environments, gates, canary, rollback & Application release and risk sign-off \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Keep contracts stable while frameworks change}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/portability.pdf}{Figure 7-2 — Platform contracts with replaceable framework and provider adapters}{portability}

Use AgentSpec, RunContext, TaskEnvelope, ToolManifest/ToolCall, ContextManifest, MemoryRecord, EvaluationResult, and ReleaseManifest as stable contracts. Compile them into framework/provider adapters. Standardized contracts, MCP, APIs, events, agent-to-agent protocols, gateways, and shared platform services provide interoperability without leaking vendor-native message objects into applications. Compatibility suites verify cancellation, tools, streaming, approvals, errors, telemetry, usage, and version behavior.\par

\section{How a capability moves from idea to production}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/developer-lifecycle.pdf}{Figure 7-3 — Local-to-production agent lifecycle}{developer-lifecycle}

Create from an approved template; attach tools, Skills, context, and memory; configure model policy, permissions, budgets, and review; test deterministic logic, contracts, simulations, adversarial cases, and failures; evaluate quality, safety, latency, and cost; package immutable artifacts with locks, SBOM, signature, provenance, and ReleaseManifest; promote through policy gates; observe correlated production traces; improve through controlled ChangeBundles.\par

\section{SDK example}

\BookSnake · illustrative pseudocode\par

\needspace{130.4pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
agent = platform.agent(
    spec="mortgage_evidence_review@0.1.0",
    model_policy="quality-balanced@7",
    permissions=["mortgage:case:read", "mortgage:evidence_request"],
    budgets={"tokens": 18_000, "usd": 0.45, "deadline_s": 90},
)
agent.attach_skill("mortgage-evidence-review@0.1.0")
agent.attach_tool(read_mortgage_case, risk="regulated", effect="evidence.read")
agent.use_context(source="mortgage-underwriting-policy", snapshot="approved-2026.09")
agent.use_memory(policy="case-evidence-preferences@1")
report = platform.test(agent).simulate("mortgage-evidence-regression")
platform.release(agent, require=["quality", "security", "cost", "rollback"])
\end{lstlisting}

This is deliberately platform pseudocode, not a claim that the OpenAI Agents SDK exposes \lstinline[style=bookinline]!platform.agent!, \lstinline[style=bookinline]!attach_skill!, or \lstinline[style=bookinline]!platform.release! with these method names. The repository's checked implementation uses a provider-neutral \lstinline[style=bookinline]!AgentRuntime! and translates its request into the pinned Agents SDK at the adapter boundary. The fluent API is convenience. The compiled contracts, policy decisions, tool/effect evidence, traces, and release manifest are the stable interface.\par

\section{Make the safe path the easy path}

Developer experience should compress setup and diagnosis time while making authority, versions, risk, evidence, and cost visible. A convenient SDK that hides those concerns transfers complexity to production incidents.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/dx-golden-path.pdf}{Figure 7-4 — Developer golden path from scaffold to operated release}{dx-golden-path}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.2350\linewidth}Y{0.2350\linewidth}Y{0.2350\linewidth}Y{0.2350\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Stage} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Developer action} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Platform assistance} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Evidence} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Stage} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Developer action} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Platform assistance} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Evidence} \\
\endhead
Scaffold & Select template and risk tier & AgentSpec, ownership, tests, eval, deployment skeleton & Purpose, owner, acceptance, data/effect class \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Develop & Add instructions, tools, Skills, context, workflow & Local runtime, mocks, seeded simulation, hot reload & Unit/contract tests and permissions \\
Debug & Inspect and compare run graphs & Trace viewer, state/time travel, prompt/context diff, replay & Reproducible failure and first divergence \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Test the contracts before you test the model}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/test-pyramid.pdf}{Figure 7-5 — Testing and simulation pyramid}{test-pyramid}

Deterministic units verify schemas, policy helpers, acceptance, routes, reducers, calculations, and state transitions. Contract tests verify identity, idempotency, timeout/unknown-effect semantics, protocol capability, errors, and telemetry. Component evaluations measure retrieval, tools, arguments, structured output, safety, and memory. Trajectory simulation tests planning, handoffs, loops, deadlines, cancellation, duplicates, dependency degradation, and human delay. End-to-end staging uses exact model, identity, gateway, data, and integration versions. Canary and shadow evaluation use explicit stop and rollback conditions.\par

\section{CLI and delivery workflow}

BASH · illustrative command sequence\par

\needspace{93.6pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={BASH},language=bookbash]
agentctl init mortgage-evidence-review --template regulated-workflow --risk high
agentctl dev --scenario missing-income-evidence --trace
agentctl test --unit --contracts --failure-injection
agentctl eval --dataset mortgage-evidence-regression@1 --compare production
agentctl package --lock --sbom --sign
agentctl promote --from staging --to production --canary 5%
agentctl traces diff run:failed run:accepted
agentctl rollback mortgage-evidence-review@0.1.0 --to 0.0.9
\end{lstlisting}

Use one immutable artifact across environments; bind endpoints, identities, secrets, sources, quotas, regions, and review queues at deployment. Pin SDKs, adapters, model aliases, tool schemas, Skills, MCP servers, policy bundles, evaluators, and knowledge snapshots. A model/provider swap is a behavior change—not harmless configuration.\par

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endhead
Framework portability & Public agent SDKs differ in cancellation, streaming, tool, approval, and error semantics. A shared contract does not make them interchangeable; only adapter conformance tests demonstrate parity, and any gap must be recorded as a known limitation rather than assumed away. & R29; R31; R32 \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

The discussion above draws on R29; R31; R32. Appendix K lists the complete sources and records their authority, version, and scope.\par

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Start from the accepted business outcome and walk backward through the causal run graph.

\item Find the first divergent span: input classification, source or freshness, prompt, route, model, tool, arguments, policy, effect, approval, or acceptance.

\item Diff the failing run against the nearest accepted run — prompt, context manifest, and every pinned version — before changing anything.

\item Do not edit the prompt until the evidence locates the failing responsibility. A prompt change that hides a policy or contract defect removes the signal without removing the fault.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} One immutable artifact crosses every environment; endpoints, identities, secrets, sources, quotas, regions, and review queues bind at deployment.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} SDKs, adapters, model aliases, tool schemas, Skills, MCP servers, policy bundles, evaluators, and knowledge snapshots are pinned in the release manifest.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} A model or provider swap ships as a behavior change with evaluation, canary, and rollback — never as configuration.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Adapter conformance suites cover cancellation, tools, streaming, approvals, errors, telemetry, usage, and version behavior.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Every golden-path stage emits its evidence artifact; a stage that produces no evidence is not complete.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Take one existing agent and list which of the sixteen platform capabilities it reimplements locally. Estimate the cost of replacing each with the platform service.

\item Write an adapter conformance test that fails when a framework silently drops cancellation mid-run.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
Why should platform contracts, not framework objects, be the stable interface? & Vendor message objects change between releases and differ in semantics. Contracts such as AgentSpec, ToolManifest, and ReleaseManifest keep applications, evidence, and audits stable while adapters absorb the change. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What makes a paved road adopted rather than mandated? & It removes work the team would otherwise do — identity, policy, tracing, durable execution, release — while leaving domain behavior, topology, and acceptance to the team. A road that constrains domain decisions gets bypassed. \\
Why is a model or provider swap not configuration? & Behavior, cost, latency, and failure modes all change without application code changing, so it requires the same evaluation, canary, and rollback evidence as a code release. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What does the golden path make visible that a convenient SDK hides? & Authority, versions, risk, evidence, and cost. An SDK that hides them does not remove the complexity; it relocates it to production incidents. \\
When is framework portability proven? & Only when adapter conformance tests pass across cancellation, tools, streaming, approvals, errors, telemetry, usage, and version behavior. Parity is never inferred from documentation. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

The delivery path is established; Chapter 8 packages repeatable behavior as versioned Skills with explicit contracts and lifecycle controls.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-08-package-capabilities-as-versioned-skills.png}

\BookChapter{08}{Package Capabilities as Versioned Skills}

\begin{bookcallout}[Northstar at this point]
Teams begin copying prompts and tool wrappers between agents. Results diverge, fixes do not propagate, and no owner can prove which behavior reached production. Northstar introduces a Skill as the unit of reusable capability.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Make the Skill manifest the versioned capability contract and resolve exact releases through policy and evaluation evidence.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why a prompt is not a reusable capability}

Prompts and tool wrappers become hard to govern as soon as several teams copy them. A Skill gives a meaningful capability an owner, an interface, a release, and evidence that it was tested. This chapter explains what belongs in that package and when versioning the whole capability is more useful than versioning an instruction string by itself.\par

\section{What belongs inside a Skill}

A Skill is useful when it can be reviewed, tested, versioned, and retired as a coherent capability. The prompt is only one part. The package also needs to say what inputs it accepts, what evidence it may read, which tools it can propose, what outputs it guarantees, how risk is classified, and how operators will know it is healthy. A small prompt edit can alter tool selection, data exposure, or output shape even though no application code changed.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Skill Registry and resolver & semantic discovery to policy-bound resolution & Skill family · registry partition & one Skill version & SkillManifest · SkillRequirement · SkillRelease \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{The Skill manifest}

A Skill is the smallest independently owned and versioned package that lets an agent perform a meaningful capability. Its manifest names interface schemas, instructions, eligible tools, knowledge sources, optional workflow template, policy constraints, side-effect class, observability fields, evaluation suite, owner, lifecycle, and immutable digest.\par

That list is abstract until you see one. A manifest is a declaration, not code:\par

YAML · a Skill manifest, abridged\par

\needspace{240.8pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={YAML},language=bookyaml]
skill: mortgage.evidence.read
version: 0.1.0
digest: sha256:9f3c...            # immutable; the version alias may move, this cannot
owner: mortgage-platform-team
side_effect_class: read_only      # governs which policy gates apply at resolution

interface:
  input:  MortgageEvidenceRequest@1
  output: MortgageEvidenceBundle@1

requires:
  tools:     [mortgage.case.read@1, mortgage.document.read@1]
  knowledge: [mortgage-policy@approved-2026.09]
  runtime:   {max_turns: 6, deadline_s: 45}

policy:
  data_classes: [mortgage_financial]
  regions:      [us]             # resolution fails outside these, it does not warn
  min_risk_tier: 1

evaluation:
  suite: mortgage-evidence-regression@1
  gates: {accuracy: 0.94, cross_tenant_leak: 0}
lifecycle: active
\end{lstlisting}

Three things in that file are doing more work than they appear to. The \lstinline[style=bookinline]!digest! is what a run actually pins, so an alias moving under you cannot change behavior mid-flight. The \lstinline[style=bookinline]!requires! block is exhaustive — a Skill that reaches for a tool not listed there fails, which is what makes the manifest a contract rather than documentation. And \lstinline[style=bookinline]!evaluation.gates! means promotion is decided by evidence, not by a reviewer's judgment on the day.\par

Skills separate reuse from copy-and-paste. An application requests a capability such as \lstinline[style=bookinline]!mortgage.evidence.read!; it does not embed a specific prompt and connector.\par

\subsection{Discovery is not permission}

This is the distinction the chapter turns on: conflating discovery with resolution is how governed capability quietly becomes ungoverned.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/skill-resolution.pdf}{Figure 8-1 — Discovery proposes candidates; resolution pins exactly one version, or fails}{skill-resolution}

\textbf{Discovery} is semantic and permissive. It searches a catalog for Skills that might satisfy a stated need, and it may legitimately return several. It answers \emph{what exists}.\par

\textbf{Resolution} is deterministic and restrictive. Given one requirement and the run's envelope, it filters by tenant, purpose, subject and actor grants, data class, region, risk, compatibility, release stage, health, and cost — then pins exactly one version by digest. It answers \emph{what this run may use, right now}.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries  & \color{white}\bfseries Discovery & \color{white}\bfseries Resolution \\
Input & A capability need in natural terms & A requirement plus the run envelope \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Method & Semantic search over the catalog & Deterministic policy evaluation \\
Output & Zero or more candidates & Exactly one pinned digest, or a failure \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
May a model influence it? & Yes — it can describe the need & \textbf{No} \\
Failure mode & Returns nothing useful & Fails closed, with the rule that refused \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

The asymmetry matters. Discovery may be fuzzy because nothing it returns is yet permitted. Resolution may not, because its output is what will actually run. A system that lets a model pick from discovery results has skipped the gate and is back to ungoverned capability with a registry in front of it.\par

\subsection{Skill lifecycle}

Draft → Certified → Staged → Active → Deprecated → Revoked is evidence-driven. Each transition requires an artifact rather than an approval conversation.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries State & \color{white}\bfseries Means & \color{white}\bfseries What moves it forward \\
\textbf{Draft} & Authored, not trusted & Passing the certification suite \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
\textbf{Certified} & Evidence exists; not yet deployed & A staging release decision \\
\textbf{Staged} & Running in staging, shadow, or a limited canary & Canary evidence within gates \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
\textbf{Active} & Resolvable by production runs & — \\
\textbf{Deprecated} & Still resolvable for runs already using it & The compatibility window expiring \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
\textbf{Revoked} & Not resolvable by anything new & — \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

Certification includes contract, unit, simulation, trajectory, threat, failure-injection, load, and operator-readiness checks. Deprecation retains a compatibility window for active runs — a long-running mortgage evidence review started three weeks ago must finish on the version it pinned. Revocation removes the Skill from all resolution and may trigger workflow pause or migration by risk class. It normally follows the compatibility window; revoking earlier is the one transition that can affect a run already in flight, which is why it is reserved for a defect serious enough to justify that.\par

\subsection{Skill contract surface}

\begin{itemize}

\item describe(requirement) -> candidates

\item resolve(requirement, run\_envelope) -> SkillRelease

\item invoke(step\_request) -> AgentStepResult

\item evaluate(release, dataset\_version) -> EvaluationReport

\item promote(release, stage, evidence) -> ReleaseDecision

\end{itemize}

The registry scales as a read-heavy control-plane service with immutable manifests, version indexes, cached eligibility metadata, and regional last-known-good replicas. Runtime execution remains in regional cells.\par

\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\

\section{A sensible place to start}

\subsection{Skill manifest and resolver}

\BookSnake · Resolving a skill version under policy and SemVer precedence\par

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
class SkillRegistry:
    def __init__(self) -> None:
        self._skills: dict[str, SkillManifest] = {}
        self._packs: dict[str, SkillPackManifest] = {}
        # Release records: lifecycle state per version, outside the digest.
        self._skill_state: dict[str, LifecycleState] = {}
        self._pack_state: dict[str, LifecycleState] = {}

    def register_skill(self, skill: SkillManifest) -> str:
        key = f"{skill.skill_id}@{skill.version}"
        if key in self._skills:
            raise ValueError(f"skill already exists: {key}")
        self._skills[key] = skill
        self._skill_state[key] = "draft"
        return key

    def set_skill_state(self, key: str, state: LifecycleState) -> None:
        if key not in self._skills:
            raise LookupError(f"unknown skill: {key}")
        self._skill_state[key] = state

    def register_pack(self, pack: SkillPackManifest) -> str:
        key = f"{pack.pack_id}@{pack.version}"
        if key in self._packs:
            raise ValueError(f"skill pack already exists: {key}")
        missing = set(pack.skill_versions) - self._skills.keys()
        if missing:
            raise ValueError(f"pack references unknown skills: {sorted(missing)}")
        self._packs[key] = pack
        self._pack_state[key] = "draft"
        return key

    def set_pack_state(self, key: str, state: LifecycleState) -> None:
        if key not in self._packs:
            raise LookupError(f"unknown skill pack: {key}")
        if state == "active" and any(
            self._skill_state[skill_key] != "active"
            for skill_key in self._packs[key].skill_versions
        ):
            raise ValueError("active pack must reference only active skills")
        self._pack_state[key] = state

    def resolve(self, requirement: SkillRequirement) -> SkillManifest:
        candidates = [
            skill
            for key, skill in self._skills.items()
            if self._skill_state[key] == "active"
            and requirement.capability in skill.capabilities
            and requirement.purpose in skill.allowed_purposes
            and requirement.region in skill.allowed_regions
            and requirement.risk_ceiling <= skill.risk_ceiling
            and requirement.required_data_classes <= skill.allowed_data_classes
        ]
        if not candidates:
            raise LookupError("no eligible skill")
        # Production resolution also uses evaluation, health, cost, and rollout policy.
        return max(candidates, key=lambda skill: _semver_key(skill.version))

    def resolve_pack(
        self, pack_key: str, *, allow_deprecated_for_replay: bool = False
    ) -> tuple[SkillManifest, ...]:
        try:
            pack = self._packs[pack_key]
        except KeyError as error:
            raise LookupError("unknown skill pack") from error
        permitted_pack_states = {"active"}
        permitted_skill_states = {"active"}
        if allow_deprecated_for_replay:
            permitted_pack_states.add("deprecated")
            permitted_skill_states.add("deprecated")
        if self._pack_state[pack_key] not in permitted_pack_states:
            raise LookupError("skill pack is not available for this resolution mode")
        if any(
            self._skill_state[key] not in permitted_skill_states
            for key in pack.skill_versions
        ):
            raise LookupError("skill pack contains unavailable skill")
        return tuple(self._skills[key] for key in pack.skill_versions)
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Resolve a Skill version from the recorded requirement and compare registry evidence.

\item Diff Skill manifests across a regression.

\item Verify no hidden tool, knowledge, or policy dependency exists outside the manifest.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Every Skill has an owner, immutable version, digest, lifecycle, and support window.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Inputs, outputs, side effects, risk, data, region, and observability are explicit.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Discovery is semantic; resolution is deterministic and policy-bound.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Tool and knowledge dependencies are pinned and compatible.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Contract, trajectory, threat, failure, and load evidence gate promotion.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Author a Skill manifest for mortgage evidence retrieval.

\item Define a certification suite that catches a cross-tenant retrieval defect.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
What is a Skill? & A versioned capability package combining contract, instructions, tools, knowledge, policy, workflow, tests, release evidence, and ownership. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How is Skill discovery different from resolution? & Discovery finds semantic candidates; resolution applies deterministic eligibility, compatibility, health, policy, and release rules to pin one version. \\
What makes a Skill portable? & Provider-neutral typed interfaces and artifact references, with SDK-specific adapters kept behind the runtime boundary. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How should a Skill be tested? & Contract, deterministic unit, simulation, trajectory, threat, data-boundary, failure-injection, regression, load, and canary tests. \\
When should behavior remain application-specific? & When it encodes one product transaction, unstable workflow, private data boundary, or ownership model that should not be generalized. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Individual Skills are governable; Chapter 9 composes them into domain packs without dissolving ownership or compatibility boundaries.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-09-turn-skills-into-a-coherent-domain-platform.png}

\BookChapter{09}{Turn Skills into a Coherent Domain Platform}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
Harborlight needs a complete domain experience, not isolated Skills. Evidence retrieval, document validation, income and property checks, policy explanation, and underwriter-package preparation must ship as a compatible, evaluated pack.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Ship domain behavior as immutable Skill Packs with compatibility, policy, evaluation, and promotion metadata.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why domain capabilities need to ship together}

One Skill can be useful; a domain workflow needs several Skills to agree about schemas, policy, terminology, evaluation, and operations. This chapter shows how to compose those pieces into a Skill Pack that can be promoted as one release. The payoff is visible during change: a mortgage team can roll out a compatible capability bundle instead of discovering incompatibilities in production one agent at a time.\par

\section{Compatibility, not convenience}

Shipping related Skills together is less about convenience than compatibility. Suppose document validation begins returning a new evidence schema while income verification still expects the old one. Independent releases can leave a run in the gap between them. A pack pins versions known to work together, tests shared workflow and policy as one behavior, and gives operators one unit to promote or roll back.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Skill Pack Registry and release pipeline & pack authoring to tenant/runtime use & domain pack · tenant rollout cohort & one pack release & SkillPackManifest · CompatibilityReport · EvaluationSuite \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{From one capability to a coherent domain experience}

A Skill Pack composes a coherent domain experience. Harborlight’s mortgage-evidence pack pins evidence retrieval, document validation, income and property checks, policy explanation, underwriter-package preparation, and document-request Skills. It also pins shared policy, taxonomy, knowledge strategy, workflow templates, evaluation datasets, dashboards, and runbooks.\par

A pack is not a directory of prompts. The pack manifest declares dependency constraints, schema compatibility, required runtime capabilities, data and region policy, risk ceiling, evaluation gates, release aliases, owner, and support window. A pack can expose a smaller capability surface to each agent even though the complete domain distribution contains more Skills.\par

\subsection{Installing a pack grants nothing}

The most important property of a pack is the one it does \emph{not} have. Installing a Skill Pack makes its capabilities \textbf{resolvable}; it does not make them \textbf{permitted}, and it grants no access to the data or tools those Skills use.\par

This is worth stating plainly because the intuition from package managers points the other way. Installing a library gives you its code and, transitively, its capabilities. Installing a pack gives you a catalog entry. Every Skill in it still resolves through the same policy evaluation as any other, against the installing tenant's own grants.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries Installing a pack does & \color{white}\bfseries Installing a pack does not \\
Add its Skills to the discovery catalog & Grant access to any data source \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Publish its manifests, schemas and digests & Grant any tool permission \\
Make its evaluation suites available & Bypass region or data-class policy \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Declare its dependency and risk ceiling & Change the installing tenant's grants \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

A business unit that installs the mortgage-evidence pack and has no entitlement to the income-verification source gets a pack whose income-verification Skill will not resolve for its runs. That is the correct outcome, and it is why sharing a pack across business units is a policy and data-boundary review rather than a package install.\par

\subsection{Pack governance and sharing}

Packs move through signed releases and aliases such as development, staging, and production. Tenant rollout policy can pin a version or cohort.\par

\subsection{Domain pack examples}

\begin{itemize}

\item \textbf{Mortgage evidence review:} evidence retrieval, document validation, income and property checks, policy explanation, underwriter-package preparation, document request.

\item \textbf{Customer support:} account read, entitlement, troubleshooting, refund proposal, escalation.

\item \textbf{Software delivery:} repository context, test planning, code change, review, deployment evidence.

\item \textbf{Financial close:} reconciliation, variance explanation, exception routing, controlled posting.

\end{itemize}

\section{A sensible place to start}

\subsection{Skill Pack contract}

\BookSnake · implementation excerpt\par

\needspace{231.6pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
from __future__ import annotations
import hashlib
import json
import re
from typing import Literal
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field, field_validator, model_validator
from .contracts import SkillRequirement

class SkillPackManifest(BaseModel):
    pack_id: str
    version: str
    domain: str
    owner: str
    skill_versions: tuple[str, ...]
    policy_bundle_ref: str
    evaluation_suite_ref: str
    status: Literal["draft", "active", "deprecated", "revoked"] = "draft"

    @field_validator("version")
    @classmethod
    def version_is_semver(cls, value: str) -> str:
        _semver_key(value)
        return value
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Expand the pack dependency graph and locate the incompatible version.

\item Compare pack alias history with active run manifests.

\item Run the pack-level evaluation suite on the failing tenant slice.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} The pack manifest pins exact compatible Skill and dependency versions.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Shared policy, knowledge, workflows, and evaluations are explicit artifacts.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Installation does not grant runtime authority.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Promotion, deprecation, rollback, and tenant rollout use aliases and evidence.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Pack-level failures can be isolated without disabling unrelated capabilities.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Compose a mortgage-evidence Skill Pack and define compatibility rules.

\item Design a deprecation plan for one Skill without breaking the pack.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\

\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\

Why create a Skill Pack? & To distribute a compatible domain capability set with shared policy, knowledge, workflows, evaluation, and lifecycle controls. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What must pack compatibility cover? & Skill and tool schemas, policy obligations, workflow contracts, knowledge versions, runtime and model requirements, and deprecation windows. \\
Can installing a pack grant authority? & No. Installation exposes definitions; tenant policy and each run envelope still determine eligibility and exact authorization. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How should packs be promoted? & As signed immutable releases through dev, staging, shadow, canary, production, and rollback aliases backed by evaluation evidence. \\
What is the main pack anti-pattern? & A monolithic prompt-and-tools bundle with hidden dependencies, no owner, no tests, and no independent versioning. \\
\end{longtable}

\section{Where this leads next}

The platform and capability model are complete; Chapter 10 begins the pattern sequence with the smallest bounded reasoning loop.\par

\BookPartPage{IV}{PATTERNS THAT WORK—AND THOSE THAT FAIL}{Patterns are shortcuts for thinking, but they are only helpful when their boundaries are visible. Every pattern in Chapters 10–15 places a model step inside the platform of Part III: the step receives a resolved slice of Skills and tools (Chapters 8–9), its outputs cross the six gates (Chapter 2), and the framework stays behind AgentRuntime (Chapter 3). Each chapter answers three questions: what the model may decide, what the controller owns, and how the pattern stops and is accepted. The Part starts with the smallest bounded loop, then follows routing, planning, reflection, human review, and durable work. It ends by comparing and combining these patterns, then naming the failure shapes they prevent—several corrected by mechanisms Parts V–VIII build—so a design review can ask not only “what could work?” but also “how would this fail in production?”}

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-10-use-react-without-creating-a-runaway-loop.png}

\BookChapter{10}{Use ReAct Without Creating a Runaway Loop}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} ReAct was introduced as a prompting paradigm that interleaves reasoning and action; its reported benchmark results motivate the loop, not an assertion that unrestricted production autonomy is safe. \textbf{\BookCitation{R01}}

\end{bookquote}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
A loan officer asks why a mortgage case is not ready for underwriting. One retrieval is insufficient: the system may need the application projection, an income-verification artifact, and a policy explanation. A bounded ReAct loop is appropriate because the next information need depends on the previous observation.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Use ReAct only for information-dependent next steps and impose external turn, tool, time, cost, and no-progress limits.
\end{bookcallout}

ReAct is valuable when the next question depends on the last observation. It is dangerous when the loop is allowed to decide its own limits or call tools without a clear stopping rule.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
External loop controller and acceptance engine & observation/model and model/tool & turn · tool permit · run budget & one bounded loop & AgentDecision · Observation · AgentStepResult · ReActLimits \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Use ReAct only when observations change the next step}

Use ReAct when a new observation can genuinely change the next useful question. If the sequence is already known—read the case, validate required fields, calculate a ratio, request approval—a durable workflow is clearer and usually cheaper. That avoids paying a model to rediscover a process the organization already understands. Even when ReAct is justified, the controller should stop when the loop repeats, exhausts its budget, or cannot obtain missing evidence. The tools a ReAct step can call are the slice resolved for that step from its pinned Skills (Chapter 8) and exposed through the Tool Gateway (Chapter 2); the loop never discovers tools on its own.\par

\section{Keep the feedback loop inside external limits}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/pattern-react.pdf}{Figure 10-1 — Bounded ReAct: the loop is inside, the limits are outside}{pattern-react}

Read the figure for what is \emph{not} in the loop. The controller admits each turn and owns the budgets; a mutation leaves as a proposal rather than executing inline; and the controller decides when the loop stops (limits, no progress, a terminal decision), while the acceptance engine—not the model's wording—decides whether the result is accepted (Chapter 13). Each turn follows the same sequence:\par

\begin{enumerate}

\item Load goal, authorized context, budgets, and acceptance criteria.

\item Ask for one typed action or terminal decision.

\item Validate the action at the Tool Gateway; execute reads and return mutations as proposals.

\item Sanitize and append the observation.

\item Continue, or end the activity on a terminal decision or an external rule; an answer goes to the acceptance engine.

\end{enumerate}

ReAct interleaves reasoning, action, and observation. Its value is conditional adaptivity: after reading the application projection, the agent may decide that another income source is unnecessary, or that a contradictory address requires another authorized document. The pattern becomes harmful when it substitutes an unconstrained loop for a known process.\par

The platform stores only typed decisions, tool intents, observations, evidence references, and stop reasons—not private chain-of-thought. Each observation is a typed, sanitized result or artifact reference carrying provenance, version, and classification, and it re-enters context as untrusted data (Chapter 6). The external controller maintains counters and normalized signatures. If an agent repeats the same tool and canonical arguments against the same source revision, or if accepted evidence does not change across turns, the controller records no progress and terminates or escalates. Information gain is the new accepted evidence a turn produces; a turn with none counts toward the no-progress threshold.\par

\subsection{Acceptance and failure semantics}

Each turn, the model returns one typed decision, carried in \lstinline[style=bookinline]!AgentStepResult.decision! (Chapter 3): \lstinline[style=bookinline]!invoke_tool! for a read, \lstinline[style=bookinline]!proposed_action! for a mutation, \lstinline[style=bookinline]!answer!, \lstinline[style=bookinline]!needs_input!, or \lstinline[style=bookinline]!delegate!. A read runs inside the loop; a proposed action leaves it as a proposal for the six gates of Chapter 2; \lstinline[style=bookinline]!delegate! asks the controller for a route or handoff (Chapter 11). The controller ends the activity with the model's terminal decision or with its own stop reason, \lstinline[style=bookinline]!budget_exhausted! or \lstinline[style=bookinline]!no_progress!, returned as the Chapter 3 step status \lstinline[style=bookinline]!failed! with that \lstinline[style=bookinline]!error_code! rather than as an exception; the workflow, not the loop, decides whether a stop reason opens an intervention (Chapter 14). A read that times out may be retried within the run budget. A proposed mutation is executed by the workflow outside the loop; if that execution times out, the effect is unknown and is reconciled before the loop resumes (Chapter 22).\par

\begin{bookantipattern}[Common pitfall]
Unbounded retries disguised as reasoning. When a tool fails and the model is free to try again, the loop can burn its whole budget rediscovering the same error. Return failures as typed data the model can read, cap attempts per tool (see AP-04), and make the cap a run-level budget rather than a per-call one.
\end{bookantipattern}

\subsection{Capacity model}

Plan capacity in turns per verified success (Chapter 28), not only requests per second. A loop's cost is set by its trajectory, not its prompt: each observation re-enters the context, so an n-step loop is roughly quadratic in tokens, and a cheap call that loops eight times is not cheap. Cap the step count, summarize observations before they re-enter, and track model tokens, tool calls, connector time, observation bytes, and repeated-action rate. Per-tenant permits and hard per-run limits prevent one pathological loop from consuming a worker pool. Per-loop caps do not compose across delegation; budgets must be withdrawn from the parent (AP-11).\par

\section{ReAct at a glance}

\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Do not use when} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Do not use when} \\
\endhead
Use for exploratory retrieval, diagnosis, or research with a small eligible tool set and measurable stop conditions. & Avoid for known workflows, single retrievals, protected commits, or open-ended browsing without meaningful limits. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Where it helps—and what it costs}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Choice} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Consequence} \\
Adaptivity & Handles unknown paths; increases latency, cost, and trajectory variance. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Intermediate observations & Improves grounding; increases attack surface and context pressure. \\
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Where it can fail—and how to contain it}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}

\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Control} \\
Tool ping-pong & Normalized repeat signatures and no-progress threshold. \\

\subsection{What you need to see in production}

\begin{itemize}

\item turn index

\item decision type

\item eligible tools

\item action-observation hash

\item information gain

\item remaining budgets

\item stop reason

\end{itemize}

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

The discussion above draws on R01. Appendix K lists the complete sources and records their authority, version, and scope.\par

\section{A sensible place to start}

Two versions of the same loop follow. The first is an OpenAI Agents SDK version in which the run budget lives in the tool context.\par

\BookSnake · Bounded ReAct loop with an explicit run budget\par

\needspace{526.0pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
@dataclass
class RunBudget:
    """Budget for one run. The model cannot see or change it."""

    case_id: str = load_anchor_case().case_id
    max_tool_calls: int = 3
    tool_calls: int = 0
    exhausted_at: str | None = None

    def consume(self, tool: str) -> bool:
        if self.tool_calls >= self.max_tool_calls:
            self.exhausted_at = tool
            return False
        self.tool_calls += 1
        return True


@function_tool
def search_mortgage_evidence(context: RunContextWrapper[RunBudget], query: str) -> str:
    """Search evidence for the anchor case. Refuses after the run budget."""
    if not context.context.consume("search_mortgage_evidence"):
        # Refusing *as data* keeps the loop bounded without raising: the model can
        # read this, stop, and explain itself rather than retrying blindly.
        return "budget exhausted: no further searches permitted in this run"
    hits = {
        "income": "a second pay_statement is missing",
        "property": "purchase_agreement is available",
        "title": "title evidence is not in this checkpoint",
    }
    evidence = hits.get(query, f"no mortgage evidence for {query!r}")
    return f"{context.context.case_id}: {evidence}"


def build_agent() -> Agent[RunBudget]:
    return Agent[RunBudget](
        name="Mortgage evidence research",
        instructions=(
            "Research the mortgage case using search_mortgage_evidence. "
            "If the budget is exhausted, summarize what you found and stop."
        ),
        tools=[search_mortgage_evidence],
    )


async def run(question: str, *, budget: RunBudget, model=None) -> str:
    result = await Runner.run(
        build_agent(),
        question,
        context=budget,
        # max_turns is the runner's own stop; the budget is the domain's. Both
        # matter: one bounds the loop, the other bounds what the loop may spend.
        max_turns=8,
        run_config=RunConfig(model=model, tracing_disabled=True),
    )
    return result.final_output
\end{lstlisting}

The second is the framework-neutral controller the chapter describes: it owns turns, tool budget, and repeat detection, runs only reads, and returns a typed stop reason instead of raising.\par

\BookSnake · A ReAct controller that owns the budgets\par

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
class ReActEngine:
    """A bounded reason-act-observe loop with an external tool boundary."""

    def __init__(
        self,
        model: DecisionModel,
        invoke_tool: ToolInvoker,
        limits: ReActLimits = ReActLimits(),
    ) -> None:
        self.model = model
        self.invoke_tool = invoke_tool
        self.limits = limits

    @staticmethod
    def _observation_fingerprint(
        tool_id: str, arguments: dict[str, object], result: dict[str, object]
    ) -> str:
        payload = json.dumps(
            {"tool_id": tool_id, "arguments": arguments, "result": result},
            sort_keys=True,
            separators=(",", ":"),
            default=repr,
        ).encode()
        return hashlib.sha256(payload).hexdigest()

    @staticmethod
    def _stop(reason: StopReason) -> AgentStepResult:
        # A tripped bound is a typed outcome, not an exception.
        return AgentStepResult(status="failed", error_code=reason)

    async def run(self, goal: str) -> AgentStepResult:
        observations: list[dict[str, object]] = []
        identical_observations: dict[str, int] = {}
        tool_calls = 0
        for _turn in range(self.limits.maximum_turns):
            decision = await self.model.decide(
                goal=goal, observations=tuple(observations)
            )
            if decision.disposition in {"answer", "delegate"}:
                return AgentStepResult(status="completed", decision=decision)
            if decision.disposition == "needs_input":
                return AgentStepResult(status="needs_input", decision=decision)
            if decision.tool_call is None:
                raise ValueError(f"{decision.disposition} decision is missing tool_call")
            if decision.disposition == "proposed_action":
                # A mutation never runs here; the workflow takes it through the gates.
                return AgentStepResult(status="proposed_action", decision=decision)
            tool_calls += 1
            if tool_calls > self.limits.maximum_tool_calls:
                return self._stop("budget_exhausted")
            result = await self.invoke_tool(
                decision.tool_call.tool_id,
                decision.tool_call.arguments,
                decision.tool_call.logical_effect_key,
            )
            observations.append(
                {"tool": decision.tool_call.tool_id, "result": result}
            )
            fingerprint = self._observation_fingerprint(
                decision.tool_call.tool_id,
                decision.tool_call.arguments,
                result,
            )
            identical_observations[fingerprint] = (
                identical_observations.get(fingerprint, 0) + 1
            )
            if (
                identical_observations[fingerprint]
                >= self.limits.maximum_identical_observations
            ):
                return self._stop("no_progress")
        return self._stop("budget_exhausted")
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item List every turn with normalized action, observation, token, time, and cost deltas.

\item Identify the first repeated action-observation signature.

\item Check whether the loop stopped on an external rule and whether acceptance came from the acceptance criteria rather than the model's wording.

\end{enumerate}

If limits reset at each delegation hop or every layer retries, see AP-11 and AP-04 in Chapter 17.\par

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} The information need truly requires iterative observation.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} The controller owns limits, state, and tool invocation; acceptance is decided outside the loop.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Observations are typed, sanitized, versioned, and provenance-linked.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Repeat-action and no-progress signatures are detected.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Unknown effects or budget exhaustion terminate safely.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Add information-gain and repeat-signature stopping to the reference ReAct loop.

\item Compare a one-shot retrieval design with ReAct for the same mortgage-evidence question.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
When is ReAct the right pattern? & When the next information need genuinely depends on a verified prior observation and the loop can be bounded. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Why must the controller sit outside the model? & It owns budgets, tool eligibility, invocation, state, no-progress detection, and terminal transitions. \\
How do you stop a ReAct loop that keeps rephrasing the same request? & Normalize action-observation signatures, count repeats, evaluate information gain, and terminate or escalate at a deterministic threshold. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What should an observation contain? & The observation contract in § Keep the feedback loop inside external limits, plus explicit error semantics. \\
When should ReAct not be used? & For a known workflow, a single retrieval, a protected transaction, or a task whose acceptance can be computed directly. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

A bounded reason–act loop handles local uncertainty. When a turn returns \lstinline[style=bookinline]!delegate!, or a known path or specialist should own the work, Chapter 11 adds routing and handoff.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-11-route-the-work-without-losing-ownership.png}

\BookChapter{11}{Route the Work Without Losing Ownership}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
Some requests are policy questions, others are document defects, evidence disputes, or protected mortgage-case actions. Harborlight needs routing, but it also needs to know when responsibility actually transfers and who owns cancellation, deadlines, and the final response.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Use deterministic route eligibility and fallback; record handoff as an explicit ownership transition.
\end{bookcallout}

Routing sounds like a classification problem until a real request is late, ambiguous, or already halfway through a workflow. This chapter separates the choice of a specialist from the transfer of responsibility, then makes fallbacks, deadlines, authority, and the final response explicit.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Route service and workflow ownership record & classifier to privileged route; owner transfer & route class · specialist queue & one route or handoff & RoutingDecision · OwnershipTransfer · CapabilityEnvelope \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Routing and handoff are different events}

A routing decision answers “who is best placed to do this?” A handoff answers “who is now accountable for finishing it?” Those are not the same event. Agent frameworks also use the word handoff for passing a conversation to another agent inside one run (Chapter 3). That framework handoff moves control, not accountability. It becomes a handoff in this chapter's sense only when the platform records the transfer and the recipient acknowledges it. If a specialist never acknowledges the task, the original coordinator still owns the deadline and user response. Recording the transfer prevents work from disappearing into a queue while every component assumes someone else has it.\par

\section{A routing decision cannot grant permission}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/pattern-routing.pdf}{Figure 11-1 — Routing selects a specialist from an eligible set; authorization decides whether it may act}{pattern-routing}

A router reduces heterogeneity by selecting an implementation: policy explanation, document validation, income verification, or human triage. A ReAct step that returns \lstinline[style=bookinline]!delegate! (Chapter 10) arrives here as a routing request. The model may classify semantic intent, but the eligible set is assembled first from purpose, authority, risk, data region, release status, service health, and capacity—deterministically, before any model sees the options, the same way Skill resolution works (Chapter 8). A route cannot grant a capability that was absent from the run's capability envelope (Chapter 21). Choosing which model serves a step follows the same eligibility-first rule but belongs to the LLM Gateway (Chapter 4), and model-routing economics to Chapter 28. In order:\par

\begin{enumerate}

\item Build the eligible route set.

\item Classify the request and return route, confidence, and reason.

\item Validate thresholds and constraints.

\item Select the route or deterministic fallback.

\item Measure outcome by route and slice.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Confidence and fallback}

Confidence is calibrated on a versioned domain dataset. Below a threshold, the route service requests clarification, applies a deterministic safe default, or sends the request to a human triage queue (Chapter 14). High confidence does not bypass policy. For privileged routes, both route eligibility and exact invocation authorization are required.\par

\section{Handoff transfers accountability}

A handoff is needed when another agent or human must become the current interaction or task owner rather than merely provide an answer to the original controller. Frameworks make it easy for one agent to involve another, but the word hides different ownership models.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Case} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{What happens} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Correct abstraction} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Case} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{What happens} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Correct abstraction} \\
\endhead
Specialist as a tool & The evidence agent asks a sanctions specialist for analysis and retains responsibility for the final disposition & Agent-as-tool or equivalent local delegation \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Framework handoff (control only) & A triage agent transfers the bounded conversation to an income-verification specialist; control changes locally but the same activity remains accountable & Framework handoff, with the same external budget and cancellation boundary \\
Accountable handoff (this chapter) & A document-defect specialist takes over the case's evidence follow-up, including its deadline and the final response to the loan officer & Recorded, acknowledged ownership transfer \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Durable delegation & A fraud investigation needs independent ownership, waits, retries, deadlines, human review, or a cross-service trust boundary & Durable \lstinline[style=bookinline]!TaskEnvelope! and child workflow (Chapter 18) \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

An accountable handoff changes the current owner of an interaction or task. The current owner creates a typed transfer that records old owner, new owner, state version, unresolved obligations, artifact references, child capability envelope, deadline, cancellation lineage, and acceptance contract.\par

\begin{enumerate}

\item Decide that ownership—not just expertise—must transfer.

\item Persist the handoff proposal.

\item Validate recipient, authority, compatibility, and capacity.

\item Require the recipient to acknowledge or reject; until then, the current owner stays accountable.

\item On acknowledgment, atomically change owner and emit an event; on rejection or timeout, keep the current owner.

\end{enumerate}

A transfer is proposed, then acknowledged, rejected, or expired; only acknowledgment changes the owner. Chapter 18 implements this protocol as the Ownership Service and its \lstinline[style=bookinline]!tasks/{id}:handoff! and \lstinline[style=bookinline]!:accept! operations; Chapter 21 explains how the child capability envelope is derived.\par

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}
