\section{A sensible place to start}

The first listing uses the SDK's framework handoff to switch agents within one run. It moves control, not accountability; the platform records ownership around it (see § Handoff transfers accountability).\par

\BookSnake · Framework handoff: passing control to a specialist within one run\par

\needspace{351.2pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
evidence_specialist = Agent(
    name="Mortgage evidence specialist",
    handoff_description="Handles missing and contradictory mortgage evidence.",
    instructions=(
        "Review mortgage evidence gaps. Do not make or imply a lending decision."
    ),
)

policy_specialist = Agent(
    name="Mortgage policy specialist",
    handoff_description="Explains the policy that governs mortgage evidence review.",
    instructions=(
        "Explain the applicable mortgage policy and cite the policy revision. "
        "Do not make or imply a lending decision."
    ),
)

# The triage agent owns routing only. It deliberately has no domain tools, so it
# cannot quietly answer a question it should have handed off.
triage = Agent(
    name="Triage",
    instructions=(
        "Route the request to the specialist that owns it. "
        "Do not answer domain questions yourself."
    ),
    handoffs=[evidence_specialist, policy_specialist],
)


async def run(request: str, *, model=None):
    result = await Runner.run(
        triage, request, max_turns=5,
        run_config=RunConfig(model=model, tracing_disabled=True),
    )
    # last_agent is the audit answer to "who actually decided this?"
    return result.final_output, result.last_agent.name
\end{lstlisting}

The second computes the eligible set before the model proposes a route, then validates the proposal and falls back instead of guessing.\par

\BookSnake · Eligibility-first routing with validation and fallback\par

\needspace{360.4pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
class DeterministicRouter:
    """Eligibility first, model proposal second, deterministic validation last."""

    def __init__(
        self, routes: tuple[Route, ...], fallback: str, threshold: float = 0.8
    ) -> None:
        self.routes = routes
        self.fallback = fallback  # e.g. a human triage queue; always available
        self.threshold = threshold

    def eligible(
        self, allowed_capabilities: frozenset[str], risk: RiskClass
    ) -> tuple[Route, ...]:
        return tuple(
            route for route in self.routes
            if route.capability in allowed_capabilities
            and risk <= route.risk_ceiling
            and route.healthy
        )

    async def route(
        self,
        request: str,
        classify: Classifier,
        allowed_capabilities: frozenset[str],
        risk: RiskClass,
    ) -> RoutingDecision:
        names = tuple(r.name for r in self.eligible(allowed_capabilities, risk))
        if not names:
            return RoutingDecision(self.fallback, True, "no_eligible_route", names)
        proposal = await classify(request, names)
        if proposal.route not in names:
            # Selecting a route is never authorization: outside the set, fall back.
            return RoutingDecision(self.fallback, True, "not_eligible", names)
        if proposal.confidence < self.threshold:
            return RoutingDecision(self.fallback, True, "low_confidence", names)
        return RoutingDecision(proposal.route, False, proposal.reason, names)
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Recompute the eligible route set from recorded purpose, authority, health, and risk.

\item Inspect the confidence threshold and fallback actually used.

\item Confirm the old owner remained accountable until the recipient's acknowledgment was recorded, and that cancellation reached the current owner.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} The eligible route set is computed before model selection.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Confidence, ambiguity, fallback, and default-deny behavior are explicit.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Routing and handoff are separate contracts.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Ownership changes only on recorded acknowledgment; state, narrowed authority, deadlines, and cancellation move with it atomically.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Route decisions and outcomes are measured by slice.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Design a route contract with confidence, reason codes, fallback, and version.

\item Model a handoff that transfers a task without broadening authority.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
How does routing differ from a handoff? & Routing selects an eligible implementation; handoff transfers responsibility and therefore requires ownership, state, authority, and cancellation semantics. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Should the model see every route? & No. Give it a minimal eligible set derived from purpose, authority, health, region, risk, and release policy. \\
What should happen below a confidence threshold? & Use a deterministic default, request clarification, or route to a human queue; do not select a privileged specialist by guesswork. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
When is a handoff the wrong choice? & When the parent still owns the outcome (agent-as-tool) or the work needs independent recovery without conversational transfer (durable child task, Chapter 18). \\
When is a static router better? & When classes are stable, risk is high, compliance requires predictability, or a simple rule already separates the work. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Routing selects among known capabilities; Chapter 12 addresses tasks whose decomposition must itself be proposed and validated.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-12-turn-proposed-plans-into-safe-executable-work.png}

\BookChapter{12}{Turn Proposed Plans into Safe, Executable Work}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} Published engineering guidance distinguishes predefined workflows from model-directed agents and recommends adding complexity only when simpler compositions are insufficient. \textbf{\BookCitation{R27}}

\end{bookquote}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
A mortgage application with a missing second pay statement, an asset discrepancy, and a property check requires adaptive sequencing. Harborlight lets a planner propose a graph but compiles that graph before any node becomes schedulable.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Use a typed Plan IR and deterministic compiler; freeze committed past work across plan revisions.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why a plausible plan still needs to be compiled}

Planning earns its complexity only when the work cannot be known in advance. When a stable route exists, route deterministically (Chapter 11); invoke the planner only at entry, at material uncertainty, or after a failure invalidates the current plan. Even then, a model-proposed plan is not permission to run arbitrary steps. This chapter treats the plan as an intermediate representation: validate it, check it stays within the run's authority, compile it into executable nodes, and freeze the work that has already committed. The distinction matters whenever the plan changes halfway through a long-running case.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Plan Service and durable workflow & planner proposal to deterministic compiler & plan node · workload queue & one node and one plan revision & CandidatePlan · CompiledPlan · PlanRevision \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Treat planning like compilation—not authorization}

The compiler analogy is useful because a plan has the same gap as source code: it may express a sensible intention and still be unsafe or impossible to execute. Models confuse a coherent narrative with a feasible, authorized execution graph. The plan compiler resolves capabilities, checks dependencies, rejects cycles, attaches budgets and deadlines, and verifies that each step stays inside the run's capability envelope (Chapter 21). A compiled plan proves its steps fit inside the envelope; it does not authorize them. Each dispatch still receives an exact authorization decision at the Tool Gateway (Chapter 2) against the current resource version, because a case version, policy decision, or approval can become stale before a later step begins.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/pattern-planning.pdf}{Figure 12-1 — A candidate plan becomes typed IR and is validated before any step runs}{pattern-planning}

The planner describes desired work; the compiler determines whether that work is legal and executable. Plan IR nodes name capability requirements rather than concrete credentials. Compilation binds each capability requirement to an eligible Skill and tool release (Chapter 8), calculates critical path and maximum cost, and emits an immutable plan revision signed by the compiler so the scheduler can verify that what runs is what was validated.\par

Resource feasibility matters. A valid graph can still miss the deadline if it requires too many browser sessions, serialized vendor calls, or human approvals. The compiler checks workload queues, concurrency classes, rate limits, regional placement, and time windows before committing a plan. Scheduling remains a workflow responsibility.\par

The alternatives run from a fixed workflow, rules engine, and router through plan-and-execute, ReAct, graph search, and planner/executor/critic to code generation. Fixed workflows maximize predictability; dynamic planning increases coverage at a higher evaluation, security, and cost burden.\par

\section{What should be agentic?}

The same split decides every pattern in this Part; in a plan it is encoded as the node \lstinline[style=bookinline]!kind!.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Work} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Recommended mechanism} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Reason} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Work} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Recommended mechanism} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Reason} \\
\endhead
Interpret ambiguous intent & LLM with structured output & Semantic ambiguity is the value of the model \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Retrieve and synthesize heterogeneous evidence & LLM + governed retrieval & Requires semantic selection and explanation \\
Propose decomposition or hypotheses & LLM planner & Subtasks are not fully known in advance \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Choose among a small stable set of routes & Rules/classifier first; LLM fallback & Lower cost and variance for common cases \\
Validate types, required fields, ranges, invariants & Deterministic code/schema & Must be complete, fast, and reproducible \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Authorization and tenant checks & Policy engine & Security decisions cannot rely on persuasion \\
Calculate money, eligibility, limits, dates & Domain service/rules engine & Precision, auditability, regulatory ownership \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Commit records, payments, deployments & Deterministic service & Transaction and concurrency semantics matter \\
Retries, timers, joins, cancellation & Workflow engine & Distributed-systems control, not language reasoning \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Content classification at ambiguous edge & Dedicated model plus deterministic thresholds & Useful probabilistic signal; policy remains external \\
Summarization and user explanation & LLM grounded in evidence & Natural-language generation is appropriate \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Acceptance tests and reconciliation & Code/tests; model judge only as a supplement & Avoid self-certification and judge bias \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Plan IR}

JSON · implementation excerpt\par

\needspace{194.8pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={JSON},language=bookjson]
{
  "plan_id": "plan_...",
  "revision": 3,
  "goal": "...",
  "capability_envelope_id": "cap_...",
  "budget": {"usd": 12, "steps": 40, "wall_clock_seconds": 7200},
  "nodes": [{
    "id": "n7",
    "kind": "llm_activity | tool_activity | child_task | gate | wait | join",
    "capability_ref": "tool://crm/get-account@2.1.0",
    "depends_on": ["n4"],
    "input_refs": ["artifact://..."],
    "output_schema": "schema://account-summary@1",
    "risk_class": "R1",
    "retry_policy": "read-transient-v2",
    "acceptance_checks": ["schema", "freshness", "tenant"],
    "on_failure": "retry | fallback | partial | escalate | compensate | fail"
  }]
}
\end{lstlisting}

The shared contract models \lstinline[style=bookinline]!CandidatePlan! and \lstinline[style=bookinline]!PlanNode! (\lstinline[style=bookinline]!examples/contracts.py!) define these fields; the compiler at the end of this chapter checks them.\par

Each node kind is executed by a mechanism defined elsewhere: \lstinline[style=bookinline]!llm_activity! is a bounded model step (Chapter 10) whose output is accepted as in Chapter 13; \lstinline[style=bookinline]!tool_activity! crosses the six gates (Chapter 2) with effect semantics from Chapter 22; \lstinline[style=bookinline]!gate! is a human approval (Chapter 14); \lstinline[style=bookinline]!wait! is a durable timer or signal (Chapter 15); \lstinline[style=bookinline]!child_task! and \lstinline[style=bookinline]!join! are durable delegation and fan-in (Chapter 18).\par

\lstinline[style=bookinline]!risk_class! uses the book's five effect classes: R0 pure transform, R1 low-sensitivity read, R2 sensitive read or reversible draft, R3 external communication or material reversible change, R4 irreversible, privileged, or financial action, where the agent may only propose (full controls in Chapter 22 § Risk classes).\par

\subsection{Compile-time checks}

\begin{itemize}

\item Every node has a pinned implementation or an allowlisted runtime selector, typed inputs/outputs, timeout, retry owner, budget, risk class, acceptance rule, and terminal failure path. A pack Skill the run may not resolve is refused here, naming the rule, never mid-run (Chapter 9).

\item Every capability lies inside the run's capability envelope. A dynamic node may choose only among an allowlisted capability set; it cannot call arbitrary network endpoints.

\item Data-flow classification can stay equal or become more restrictive. A node cannot send restricted data to a capability whose manifest/region/purpose is incompatible.

\item Mutating nodes require idempotency/effect semantics (Chapter 22) and any policy-required preview, approval (Chapter 14), or compensation. An R4 (irreversible or privileged) commit remains a deterministic domain operation.

\item Loops require a deterministic guard plus maximum iterations, wall time, cost, and a no-progress detector using the no-progress signatures of Chapter 10; unbounded loops and unhandled terminal paths are rejected. Delegation depth and parallel fan-out are statically bounded; see AP-11 for why per-child limits do not compose.

\item The compiler estimates critical path, maximum fan-out, worst-case calls/cost, and required quota. Admission (Chapter 28) may reject, defer, or require approval before commit.

\end{itemize}

\section{Revise without rewriting history}

New evidence can invalidate future work. Replanning starts from an explicit set of immutable facts: accepted artifacts, committed effects, approval decisions, and current resource versions. The new revision includes a diff, rationale, superseded nodes, reused artifacts, changed budget, and compiler evidence. It cannot rewrite history.\par

\begin{enumerate}

\item Persist the triggering observation/failure and current state version.

\item Freeze completed effects and accepted artifacts; mark nodes whose assumptions changed as invalidated.

\item Ask the planner only for the affected horizon, not the entire run.

\item Compile and diff the candidate against the committed revision.

\item Reuse nodes only when implementation, inputs, policy, data revisions, and acceptance remain compatible.

\item Obtain a new approval (Chapter 14) if risk, purpose, destination, spend, or authority expands.

\item Atomically commit the revision and reservations, then schedule newly ready nodes.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Scalability and performance}

\begin{itemize}

\item Planner workers are stateless and queue driven. Plan storage is partitioned by tenant and run; immutable revisions, schemas, and large graph blobs are stored or cached by digest, while policy and health are rechecked at commit and dispatch.

\item Validate incrementally: a plan revision carries changed nodes/edges, allowing graph, type, cost, and policy analysis to recompute only impacted regions.

\item The scheduler scans no global table. Workflow events enqueue newly ready node IDs; conditional updates ensure each logical node is dispatched once per accepted attempt.

\item Apply per-run ready-node limits and weighted fair scheduling across tenants. Planning cannot reserve unbounded future capacity; reservations expire and are reclaimed.

\end{itemize}

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

\endhead
Durable scheduling & Durable-execution systems preserve workflow progress, timers, and replay across failures, which is why compiled plans are scheduled through a workflow rather than an in-process loop. & R02 \\
\end{longtable}

The discussion above draws on R27; R02. Appendix K lists the complete sources and records their authority, version, and scope.\par

\section{A sensible place to start}

The compiler below applies the checks above to a candidate plan: capability allowlist, risk ceiling, unknown dependencies, cost, cycles, and a digest of what was validated.\par

\BookSnake · Compiling a validated plan into a budget-bounded envelope\par

\needspace{599.6pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
def compile_plan(plan: CandidatePlan, envelope: PlanEnvelope) -> dict[str, object]:
    """Validate and bind a proposed plan to the policy envelope.

    Node maximum_cost_usd values are conservative reservations. Their sum is
    the compiled reservation even if runtime branches may be mutually exclusive.
    Actual spend is tracked separately and can never consume beyond the run grant.
    """

    if len(plan.nodes) > envelope.maximum_nodes:
        raise ValueError("node limit exceeded")
    by_id = {node.id: node for node in plan.nodes}
    if len(by_id) != len(plan.nodes):
        raise ValueError("duplicate node ID")

    for node in plan.nodes:
        if node.capability not in envelope.allowed_capabilities:
            raise PermissionError(f"capability not allowed: {node.capability}")
        if node.risk_class > envelope.maximum_risk:
            raise PermissionError(f"risk ceiling exceeded: {node.id}")
        unknown = set(node.depends_on) - by_id.keys()
        if unknown:
            raise ValueError(f"unknown dependencies for {node.id}: {sorted(unknown)}")

    reserved_cost = sum(
        (node.maximum_cost_usd for node in plan.nodes), Decimal("0")
    )
    if not reserved_cost.is_finite() or reserved_cost > envelope.maximum_cost_usd:
        raise ValueError("plan exceeds maximum cost")

    dependencies = {node.id: set(node.depends_on) for node in plan.nodes}
    ready = sorted(node_id for node_id, deps in dependencies.items() if not deps)
    order: list[str] = []
    while ready:
        current = ready.pop(0)
        order.append(current)
        for node_id, deps in dependencies.items():
            if current in deps:
                deps.remove(current)
                if not deps and node_id not in order and node_id not in ready:
                    ready.append(node_id)
        ready.sort()
    if len(order) != len(plan.nodes):
        raise ValueError("cycle detected")

    plan_payload = plan.model_dump(mode="json")
    envelope_payload = {
        "allowed_capabilities": sorted(envelope.allowed_capabilities),
        "maximum_cost_usd": str(envelope.maximum_cost_usd),
        "maximum_nodes": envelope.maximum_nodes,
        "maximum_risk": envelope.maximum_risk.name,
        "policy_digest": envelope.policy_digest,
    }
    return {
        "revision": plan.revision,
        "topological_order": tuple(order),
        "reserved_cost_usd": reserved_cost,
        "cost_semantics": "sum_of_per_node_maximum_reservations",
        "policy_digest": envelope.policy_digest,
        "plan_digest": _sha256(plan_payload),
        "compiled_plan_digest": _sha256(
            {"plan": plan_payload, "envelope": envelope_payload}
        ),
    }
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Re-run the plan compiler against the exact release and policy versions.

\item Find the first node whose capability, budget, or schema became invalid.

\item Compare plan revisions and ensure committed facts did not change.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Model planning is used only for genuine semantic uncertainty.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Plans are typed, immutable, versioned graphs with explicit limits and acceptance.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Graph, authority, security, resource, schema, and effect constraints compile before commit.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Completed effects and accepted artifacts are frozen across revisions.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Revisions, loops, fan-out, depth, time, cost, and no-progress behavior are bounded.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Extend the Plan IR with deadlines and fallback paths.

\item Construct a plan that passes schema validation but must fail authority validation.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
When should you use a deterministic workflow instead of model planning? & When paths, invariants, and recovery semantics are known; use planning only for real semantic uncertainty or environmental adaptation. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Why create a typed Plan IR? & It makes nodes, dependencies, capabilities, schemas, risk, budgets, and failure behavior inspectable, compilable, versionable, and schedulable. \\
What must a plan compiler validate? & Graph integrity, schema compatibility, authority subset, data and region policy, effect and approval semantics, capacity, cost, deadlines, and terminal paths. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
How should replanning handle completed work? & Treat committed effects and accepted artifacts as immutable facts; revise only the affected future horizon and append a new version with rationale and diff. \\
How do you prevent plausible but endless planning? & External revision, loop, step, cost, time, delegation, and no-progress limits plus deterministic acceptance and terminal transitions. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Planning creates an executable structure, and every plan node ends in acceptance checks; Chapter 13 defines what may decide them—independent evaluation and bounded revision without self-certification.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-13-use-critique-to-improve-never-to-self-approve.png}

\BookChapter{13}{Use Critique to Improve—Never to Self-Approve}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
The first draft recommendation for the mortgage case is coherent but omits a contradictory address and overstates confidence. Harborlight adds bounded reflection for revision and an independently defined evaluator for acceptance.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Use reflection for targeted revision and independent, calibrated acceptance for protected outcomes.
\end{bookcallout}

Critique can make a draft better, but a generator should not be allowed to grade and approve its own work. This chapter gives reflection a bounded job—find a targeted defect and propose a revision—while an independently defined evaluator and acceptance engine decide whether the result is good enough. That separation controls both false confidence and runaway evaluation cost.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Acceptance Engine & generator/evaluator and evaluator/commit & candidate · scorer pool · slice & one revision cycle & EvaluationResult · Rubric · AcceptanceDecision \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Give evaluation its own independent path}

Independent evaluation does not mean asking the same model to reread its answer and declare it better. Generator and evaluator can share the same blind spot, especially when they use the same model family, context, or assumptions.\par

\section{Reflection revises; an acceptance engine decides}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/pattern-reflection.pdf}{Figure 13-1 — A bounded revise loop whose verdict is advisory; acceptance happens outside it}{pattern-reflection}

Reflection asks, “What is missing or inconsistent?” Evaluation asks, “Does this candidate meet an acceptance rubric?” In the mortgage case, the contradictory address is caught by an executable check against the application projection; whether the recommendation overstates confidence is the kind of qualitative criterion a calibrated judge may assess. Combining reflection and evaluation can improve complex artifacts, but only if the roles are explicit and bounded. The platform runs executable checks before any model judge: schema, evidence presence, policy, effect state, resource version, arithmetic, and deterministic domain rules.\par

Qualitative criteria—clarity, completeness, grounded synthesis, or policy-explanation quality—may use calibrated judges. Judge prompt, model route, examples, sampling parameters, and rubric are versioned. A judge used at runtime must already be calibrated: Chapter 25 § Layer tests from contracts to outcomes defines how judges are measured against human labels and gated for release. Inside a run, a judge score is one input to the acceptance decision; it is never permission to access data or commit a transaction (see AP-10).\par

\subsection{The acceptance decision}

The acceptance engine is the only component that may mark a candidate, a plan node (Chapter 12), or a ReAct answer (Chapter 10) as accepted. It takes the candidate, the authoritative state the candidate claims to satisfy, and a versioned set of criteria. It evaluates executable invariants first and calibrated judges only for criteria that cannot be computed. It returns an \lstinline[style=bookinline]!AcceptanceDecision!: accepted, rejected with typed defects, or escalated. For each criterion the decision records the evaluator type—deterministic, or judge with its version—and evidence references. Chapter 2 § Deterministic acceptance lists what Harborlight checks; Chapter 15 § Terminal correctness maps the decision to the run's terminal state.\par

\subsection{Bounded optimization loop}

Each revision targets structured feedback and carries a diff. In order:\par

\begin{enumerate}

\item Generate candidate v1.

\item Run deterministic checks first.

\item Run calibrated qualitative evaluation if needed.

\item Apply actionable feedback to one revision.

\item Let the acceptance engine accept, reject with defects, or escalate at a bounded threshold.

\end{enumerate}

The loop stops on pass, maximum revisions, maximum spend, deadline, or no-progress (a revision that changes wording but not the failing invariant, the same signature test as Chapter 10). If the evaluator and generator repeatedly disagree, the workflow escalates with both artifacts to a human intervention queue (Chapter 14) rather than silently choosing the more persuasive prose. The loop is runtime adaptation inside a fixed envelope: it may revise the candidate, never the rubric, judge, or thresholds (Chapter 25 § Separate runtime adaptation from offline improvement).\par

\section{Reflection and evaluator–optimizer at a glance}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}

\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Choice} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Consequence} \\
Better completeness & Additional model calls, correlated errors, and longer latency. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Structured critique & More debuggable; requires defect taxonomy and evaluation. \\
Explicit rubric & Improves governance; can overfit to benchmark or judge preferences. \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Where it can fail—and how to contain it}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Control} \\
Self-confirmation & Different rubric, evidence, or evaluator and deterministic checks. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Evidence invention & Require evidence references and reject unsupported additions. \\
Stylistic churn or endless revision & Diff limits, maximum revisions, cost, time, and no-progress stop. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Judge variance & Repeated runs and thresholds; calibration per Chapter 25. \\
Reward hacking & Holdout tests and diverse scorers; Chapter 25. \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{What you need to see in production}

\begin{itemize}

\item candidate version and diff size

\item defect taxonomy

\item evidence refs

\item scorer versions

\item per-criterion scores and judge rationale

\item quality delta and variance

\item acceptance decision and stop reason

\end{itemize}

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endhead
Evaluation tooling & Current evaluation platforms support datasets, code-based and LLM judges, and human feedback; using judges only as evidence for an independent acceptance decision is this book's recommendation. & R20 \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

The discussion above draws on R20. Appendix K lists the complete sources and records their authority, version, and scope.\par

\section{A sensible place to start}

The judge supplies evidence; only the acceptance engine decides.\par

\BookSnake · A bounded evaluator-optimizer whose judge cannot accept\par

\needspace{553.6pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
class AcceptanceEngine:
    """The only component that may accept. Checks decide; scores are evidence."""

    def __init__(self, thresholds: dict[str, float]) -> None:
        self.thresholds = thresholds

    def decide(
        self, candidate: str, failures: tuple[CheckFailure, ...],
        scores: tuple[EvaluationResult, ...],
    ) -> AcceptanceDecision:
        defects = tuple(f.invariant for f in failures) + tuple(
            s.criterion for s in scores
            if s.score < self.thresholds.get(s.criterion, 1.0)
        )
        if defects:
            return AcceptanceDecision("rejected", defects, (candidate,))
        return AcceptanceDecision("accepted", (), (candidate,))

    def escalate(
        self, previous: str, current: str, defects: tuple[str, ...], reason: str
    ) -> AcceptanceDecision:
        return AcceptanceDecision("escalated", defects, (previous, current), reason)


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RevisionLimits:
    maximum_revisions: int = 2
    maximum_cost_usd: float = 1.0


async def evaluate_and_revise(
    task: str, generate: Generator, checks: Checks, judge: Judge,
    engine: AcceptanceEngine, limits: RevisionLimits = RevisionLimits(),
) -> AcceptanceDecision:
    feedback: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    previous: str | None = None
    previous_defects: tuple[str, ...] | None = None
    spent = 0.0
    for revision in range(limits.maximum_revisions + 1):
        candidate = await generate(task, feedback)
        spent += candidate.cost_usd
        failures = checks(candidate.text)            # executable invariants first
        scores = () if failures else await judge(candidate.text)  # qualitative only
        decision = engine.decide(candidate.text, failures, scores)
        if decision.outcome == "accepted":
            return decision
        prior = previous if previous is not None else candidate.text
        if decision.defects == previous_defects:     # new wording, same failure
            return engine.escalate(prior, candidate.text, decision.defects, "no_progress")
        if spent >= limits.maximum_cost_usd:
            return engine.escalate(prior, candidate.text, decision.defects, "budget_exhausted")
        if revision == limits.maximum_revisions:
            return engine.escalate(prior, candidate.text, decision.defects, "maximum_revisions")
        feedback = tuple(f"{f.invariant}: {f.detail}" for f in failures) + tuple(
            f"{s.criterion}: {s.rationale}" for s in scores
        )
        previous, previous_defects = candidate.text, decision.defects
    raise AssertionError("unreachable: the last revision always returns")
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Compare generator and evaluator evidence to detect shared blind spots.

\item Inspect score variance across repeated judge runs and slices.

\item Detect revisions that change wording but not the failing invariant.

\end{enumerate}

If the model's own "done" or a judge score is the acceptance decision, see AP-07 and AP-10 in Chapter 17.\par

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Generator and evaluator roles, prompts, models, and evidence are distinguishable.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Computable invariants use code, not a model judge.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Revision count, spend, time, and no-progress are bounded.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Judge calibration and inter-rater agreement are measured (Chapter 25).\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Failed candidates and feedback enter a regression corpus (Chapter 25).\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Add a deadline stop to the evaluator–optimizer example and test that it escalates with both candidate versions.

\item Create a case where reflection worsens a correct answer and define the stop rule.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
How is reflection different from evaluation? & Reflection proposes improvements; evaluation applies an acceptance rubric and should be independently calibrated or deterministic where possible. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Why is self-evaluation dangerous? & The same model can repeat the same blind spots and produce confident rationales without independent evidence. \\
How do you bound evaluator–optimizer? & Limit revisions, cost, time, and accepted feedback types; stop on pass, no-progress, or escalation. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What should always be checked deterministically? & Schema, policy, authority, evidence presence, effect state, resource versions, arithmetic, and other computable invariants. \\
When should you not add reflection? & When the first result already meets an executable specification, latency is critical, or critique adds no measured improvement. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Automated evaluation can improve a candidate but cannot settle every decision. When the evaluator and generator disagree, or the action is protected, Chapter 14 puts a person where their judgment matters.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-14-put-people-where-their-judgment-matters.png}

\BookChapter{14}{Put People Where Their Judgment Matters}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} Human-review interfaces should be tested against WCAG 2.2 accessibility criteria; accessibility conformance does not by itself eliminate automation bias or establish decision fairness. \textbf{\BookCitation{R41}}

\end{bookquote}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
A protected mortgage-case operation requires human approval. The reviewer must see the exact operation, destination, evidence, expected resource version, and risk—not a vague request to “approve the mortgage.”
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Persist proposal-bound approvals as durable workflow events and reauthorize immediately before commit.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why human review must change system state}

Human review is most useful when it resolves a decision the system cannot or should not make alone. It becomes theater when the reviewer sees a summary that can change after approval, or when a click is detached from the effect it approves. This chapter binds approval to an immutable proposal, evidence, identity, expiry, and a durable state transition.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Workflow plus Approval Service & machine proposal to human authority & approval and intervention queues · risk class · tenant & one immutable proposal & ApprovalRequest · ApprovalDecision · InterventionTask · EffectReceipt \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Separate approval, intervention, and operator control}

Three human controls carry different authority. \textbf{Approval} comes before an effect: it asks whether one exact proposal may proceed, so the interface shows the immutable proposal, evidence, consequences, and expiry. \textbf{Intervention} is raised by the system when a run cannot finish alone: a person diagnoses, corrects, supplies information, or chooses among unresolved alternatives, so the interface exposes uncertainty and viable alternatives. \textbf{Operator control} is a person acting on a run in flight through governed commands. Intervention and operator control both change a running run; neither approves an effect. Each control gets its own queue, role, form, SLO, and terminal semantics, because one generic approval queue encourages rubber-stamping and makes the audit record difficult to interpret.\par

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/pattern-hitl.pdf}{Figure 14-1 — Approval and intervention: different triggers, different authority, different evidence}{pattern-hitl}

The Approval Service stores an immutable canonical proposal, which becomes invalid when any protected field changes, and the commit path rechecks it (§ Approval contract and § Records worth preserving give the fields). Chapter 3 showed that an SDK pause is only an interruption; this record is what turns it into an approval.\par

Human capacity is finite. Arrival rate, service time, queue age, dwell, abandonment, reassignment, rejection, correction, disagreement, and after-hours coverage are production metrics. A system that requires routine approval for low-risk deterministic work may be less safe because reviewers become fatigued.\par

\section{Design human review as part of the system}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required analysis} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Design conclusion} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required analysis} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Design conclusion} \\
\endhead
Problem & Humans must understand, approve, correct, or stop consequential work without becoming a rubber stamp or breaking run integrity. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Why it is difficult & Too many approvals cause fatigue; too little context prevents informed decisions; approvals can go stale as data or plans change; edits must be attributable and replayable. \\
Design alternatives & Confirm every tool call; risk-based approvals; maker-checker; sampled review; exception-only review; fully manual commit. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Recommended architecture & Use policy-triggered gates based on risk, novelty, confidence, value, data sensitivity, reversibility, and destination. Present a concise preview, evidence, alternatives, changed fields, policy rationale, and blast radius. An ApprovalDecision approves a specific immutable proposal for a limited time. \\
Trade-offs & Human review adds latency and staffing cost but provides judgment and accountability where automation evidence is insufficient. Poor UX converts the control into theater. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Failure scenarios & Approval applies to a changed plan; approver lacks authority; agent hides uncertainty; request times out; two operators issue conflicting overrides; “approve all” becomes normal. \\
Enterprise example & A mortgage agent drafts a request to obtain the missing second pay statement. The underwriter sees the policy basis, case evidence, requested document, downstream effect, and alternatives; any case, document, or destination change invalidates the approval. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Approval contract}

An ApprovalRequest contains what the approver must see: the proposal hash over the exact action and arguments, the resource and its \lstinline[style=bookinline]!logical_effect_key!, the before/after diff, subject and actor, evidence links, uncertainty, the policy rule and its \lstinline[style=bookinline]!policy_digest!, risk class, estimated impact or cost, the reversible flag, the \lstinline[style=bookinline]!expected_resource_version!, expiry, the separation-of-duties requirement, and the allowed responses (approve, reject, edit-and-resubmit, request-evidence, cancel-run).\par

Approving a plan (Chapter 12) approves its scope, not its effects. Each protected effect still receives its own proposal-bound approval, unless the approved plan already fixed that effect's canonical proposal and the plan revision is unchanged at commit.\par

\subsection{Intervention contract}

Every Part IV escalation lands in the intervention queue as a typed InterventionTask: no progress or an exhausted budget in a ReAct loop (Chapter 10), a route below threshold sent to human triage (Chapter 11), a plan that fails compilation or revision (Chapter 12), a generator and evaluator that keep disagreeing (Chapter 13), and a run that reaches InterventionRequired or holds an Unknown effect that reconciliation cannot settle (Chapters 15 and 22). The reviewer sees the uncertainty, the competing artifacts or alternatives, the current state version, and what is blocked. The allowed responses are to supply information (a typed signal to the workflow), choose an alternative (a plan revision under Chapter 12's rules), correct state (a governed update with an expected version), reassign, cancel, or declare an operational resolution with a reason. Responses use the run's signal and update endpoints (Chapter 4). An intervention response is an input to the run; it never approves an effect, and any protected effect it leads to still needs a proposal-bound approval.\par

\subsection{Operator console requirements}

Operator control is the third human role: a person acts on a run in flight, and every action is a governed command recorded against the run.\par

\begin{itemize}

\item Live run graph (Chapter 24) with current owner, state, budgets, queues, waits, child tasks, and last committed effect.

\item Redacted but causally complete trace; model/tool inputs available only to authorized incident roles.

\item Pause, cancel, retry safe activity, skip with justification, patch plan, reassign approval, increase budget, trigger compensation (Chapter 22), and fork a sandbox replay (Chapter 24).

\item Conflict detection and optimistic versions for operator edits.

\item Immutable operator identity, reason, timestamp, before/after state, and linked ticket on every override.

\item SLA timers and escalation paths for every queue.

\item Review and intervention forms are tested against WCAG 2.2 \BookCitation{R41}. Conformance does not remove automation bias, so measure it: reviewer agreement on seeded cases and override rate.

\end{itemize}

\section{A workable implementation}

\subsection{Who should own each decision}

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\subsection{Records worth preserving}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entity} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required fields and invariants} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entity} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required fields and invariants} \\
\endhead
ApprovalRequest & approval ID, tenant/run/node/effect, required approver predicate/count, state/version, plus the contract fields above \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
ApprovalEvidenceSnapshot & approval, immutable source/artifact refs and hashes, policy/rationale, uncertainty, alternatives, impact, redaction profile, created version \\
ApprovalAssignment & approval, queue, eligible-role predicate, assignee/claim lease, SLA, escalation level, state \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
ApprovalDecision & approval, proposal hash, approver identity/role/assurance, decision, reason, signature/attestation, time; append-only \\
InterventionTask & run/node, trigger and the source chapter's reason code, evidence refs, alternatives, allowed responses, assignee, SLA, state/version, response; append-only \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
OperatorAction & action ID, run, command, actor/role, target/version, before/after refs, reason, linked ticket, result/effect; append-only \\
EscalationPolicy & queue/risk/value, warning/expiry timers, fallback roles, business calendar, timeout disposition; implicit approval is forbidden \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
NotificationDelivery & approval/action, channel, destination handle, idempotency key, attempts, delivered/acknowledged, error; contains no unredacted evidence \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

A decision is valid only when the presented proposal hash equals the current proposal, the approver is authorized at decision time, required counts/separation rules are satisfied, and the approval has not expired or been superseded. A valid approval proves authority over this proposal; it does not stop a duplicate or a stale overwrite. Chapter 22 pairs it with idempotency, expected versions, and receipts.\par

\subsection{Workflow integration}

\begin{enumerate}

\item The workflow persists the proposed effect and evidence snapshot, then creates the approval.

\item The Approval Service registers durable SLA/expiry timers and returns a wait correlation ID.

\item Assignment and notifications may retry independently; the workflow remains suspended without a worker.

\item A decision transaction records the immutable decision and emits an outbox event.

\item The workflow receives the event, revalidates policy/current resource version, and commits only the proposal whose hash was approved.

\item If the resource, amount, recipient, scope, or policy changed materially, the workflow supersedes the approval and generates a fresh preview. Chapter 15 generalizes this wait to every timer, signal, and callback.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{What changes as usage grows}

\begin{itemize}

\item Serialize decisions per approval, scale across approvals, and drive SLA timers from durable timers (Chapter 15), never table scans.

\item Materialize queue views by eligibility dimensions and age. Compute final authorization at read/claim/decision time so an index cannot leak work after role changes.

\item Coalesce notifications and support digest/batched review for low-risk homogeneous actions, but preserve one proposal and decision record per effect.

\item Approval capacity is part of end-to-end capacity planning; size queues and staffing from the production metrics above.

\item Prevent approval stampedes during incidents with per-queue rate limits, prioritized critical items, bulk-action policy, and explicit incident-mode controls—never an unbounded “approve all.”

\item Decisions are written only in the run's home region (Chapter 27 covers failover fencing).

\end{itemize}

\begin{bookproduction}[Production consideration]
Design the wait, not just the prompt. A run paused for approval may sit for days across deploys, token expiry, and staff changes. Persist the full decision context with the pause.
\end{bookproduction}

\section{Approval and intervention at a glance}

An approval follows § Approval contract; an intervention is an InterventionTask as defined in § Intervention contract. Both are durable waits that resume through authenticated signals.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart

\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Use when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Do not use when} \\
\endhead
Use for high-risk, irreversible, regulated, ambiguous, or policy-exception decisions. & Do not add a cosmetic click for already-automated low-risk steps or ask a human to review unreadable raw model traces. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{What you need to see in production}

\begin{itemize}

\item proposal hash

\item risk and policy basis

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
\endhead
Approval integrity & Book-authored recommendation, informed by R15's mitigation guidance for agentic applications: approve the exact canonical proposal, not a natural-language summary, and revalidate policy, authority, proposal hash, expiry, destination, and resource version immediately before commit. & R15 \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Review interfaces & Review and intervention forms should meet WCAG 2.2 success criteria; conformance does not by itself remove automation bias. & R41 \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

The discussion above draws on R15; R41. Appendix K lists the complete sources and records their authority, version, and scope.\par

\section{A sensible place to start}

The first example persists the wait and verifies a signed, expiring decision; the second is the commit-time check that the decision still covers this exact proposal and current state.\par

\BookSnake · A human approval gate that survives the wait\par

\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
def proposal_hash(action: str, arguments: dict) -> str:
    """Bind an approval decision to one exact proposal, not to an intent."""
    payload = json.dumps({"action": action, "arguments": arguments}, sort_keys=True)
    return hashlib.sha256(payload.encode()).hexdigest()


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ApprovalDecision:
    proposal_hash: str
    approver_id: str
    expires_at: datetime
    signature: str


class ApprovalExpired(Exception):
    """The approval decision lapsed before the effect committed."""


class ApprovalMismatch(Exception):
    """The proposal changed after it was approved."""


class ApprovalNotAuthentic(Exception):
    """The approval decision was not issued by the Approval Service."""


class ApprovalService:
    """Issues signed decisions. Only this component speaks for a human reviewer."""

    def __init__(self, secret: bytes) -> None:
        self._secret = secret

    def _signature(self, proposal_hash: str, approver_id: str, expires_at: datetime) -> str:
        payload = f"{proposal_hash}|{approver_id}|{expires_at.isoformat()}".encode()
        return hmac.new(self._secret, payload, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest()

    def issue(self, proposal_hash: str, approver_id: str, expires_at: datetime) -> ApprovalDecision:
        return ApprovalDecision(
            proposal_hash=proposal_hash, approver_id=approver_id, expires_at=expires_at,
            signature=self._signature(proposal_hash, approver_id, expires_at),
        )

    def verify(self, decision: ApprovalDecision) -> None:
        expected = self._signature(decision.proposal_hash, decision.approver_id, decision.expires_at)
        # compare_digest, not ==: an equality check on a signature leaks timing.
        if not hmac.compare_digest(expected, decision.signature):
            raise ApprovalNotAuthentic("decision was not issued by the Approval Service")


@dataclass
class PendingApprovals:
    """Persisted pauses. A real system stores these in the workflow database."""

    service: ApprovalService
    pending: dict[str, dict] = field(default_factory=dict)

    def pause(self, run_id: str, action: str, arguments: dict, ttl: timedelta) -> str:
        # Deep copy: the caller keeps a reference, and a nested value mutated
        # after the pause would otherwise change the proposal a real effect uses
        # without changing the digest that was approved.
        arguments = copy.deepcopy(arguments)
        self.pending[run_id] = {
            "digest": proposal_hash(action, arguments),
            "action": action,
            "arguments": arguments,
            # The context is stored with the pause. Reconstructing it later from
            # a transcript is exactly what fails after a deploy.
            "expires_at": datetime.now(timezone.utc) + ttl,
        }
        return self.pending[run_id]["digest"]

    def commit(self, run_id: str, decision: ApprovalDecision, now: datetime | None = None) -> str:
        record = self.pending[run_id]
        now = now or datetime.now(timezone.utc)
        # Authenticity first: an unsigned decision is not an approval, so there is
        # nothing to compare it against.
        self.service.verify(decision)
        # Recompute from the stored proposal rather than trusting the stored
        # digest, so tampering with the pending record is caught too.
        current = proposal_hash(record["action"], record["arguments"])
        if decision.proposal_hash != current:
            raise ApprovalMismatch("the approved proposal is not the one being committed")
        if now >= decision.expires_at or now >= record["expires_at"]:
            raise ApprovalExpired("approval lapsed; request it again")
        del self.pending[run_id]
        return f"committed {record['action']} approved by {decision.approver_id}"
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Verify the decision at commit}

\BookSnake · Verifying a human approval against this exact proposal\par

\needspace{452.4pt}
\begin{lstlisting}[style=bookcode,title={\BookSnake},language=Python]
class ApprovalDecision(BaseModel):
    approval_id: str
    proposal_hash: str
    tenant_id: str
    run_id: str
    approver_id: str
    approved_at: datetime
    expires_at: datetime
    policy_digest: str
    obligations: tuple[str, ...] = Field(default_factory=tuple)

    @field_validator("approved_at", "expires_at")
    @classmethod
    def timestamps_are_aware(cls, value: datetime, info) -> datetime:
        return _require_aware(value, info.field_name)

    @model_validator(mode="after")
    def expiry_follows_approval(self) -> "ApprovalDecision":
        if self.expires_at <= self.approved_at:
            raise ValueError("expires_at must be later than approved_at")
        return self


def verify_approval(
    proposal: ApprovalRequest,
    decision: ApprovalDecision,
    *,
    now: datetime,
    actual_resource_version: int,
) -> None:
    """Fail closed if the approval no longer matches the action or current state."""

    _require_aware(now, "now")
    if decision.proposal_hash != proposal.digest():
        raise PermissionError("approval is not bound to this proposal")
    if (decision.tenant_id, decision.run_id) != (proposal.tenant_id, proposal.run_id):
        raise PermissionError("approval scope mismatch")
    if decision.approver_id == proposal.actor_id:
        raise PermissionError("actor cannot self-approve")
    if decision.policy_digest != proposal.policy_digest:
        raise PermissionError("policy changed after approval")
    if now < decision.approved_at:
        raise PermissionError("approval is not yet effective")
    if now >= min(decision.expires_at, proposal.expires_at):
        raise PermissionError("approval expired")
    if actual_resource_version != proposal.expected_resource_version:
        raise PermissionError("resource changed after approval")
\end{lstlisting}

\section{If this fails in production}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Recompute the proposal hash and expected resource version.

\item Verify approver authority, expiry, and separation-of-duty policy.

\item Trace the approval event through commit and effect receipt.

\item If approval is a Boolean or a chat reply rather than a bound record, the repair is structural; see AP-06 in Chapter 17.

\end{enumerate}

\section{What to verify before production}

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Approval, intervention, and operator control use separate queues, roles, forms, and terminal semantics.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Every approval request has an expiry and an explicit timeout disposition; a timeout never becomes implicit approval.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Approval binds to an immutable proposal hash and current authority and resource state.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Every Part IV escalation opens a typed InterventionTask, and no intervention response approves an effect.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Reconciliation, compensation, pause, cancel, and intervention are governed commands.\par

\textbf{\ensuremath{\checkmark}} Reviewer queue age, dwell, abandonment, and agreement are monitored as SLOs.\par

\section{Put the idea to work}

\begin{enumerate}

\item For the Harborlight document-request proposal, list which field changes force supersession and which do not.

\item Define how edits, policy changes, and expired approvals invalidate an approval.

\item Design the intervention task raised when Chapter 13's generator and evaluator disagree twice.

\end{enumerate}

\section{Questions to bring to design review}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Question} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Strong answer} \\
\endhead
Why separate approval from intervention? & Approval asks whether one exact effect may proceed; intervention supplies missing judgment or corrects a run. Separate queues, roles, forms, and terminal semantics keep the audit record interpretable. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What happens when an approval request times out? & It follows an explicit escalation policy and timeout disposition; a timeout never becomes implicit approval. \\
How should approval be bound to an action? & Persist the exact canonical proposal, preview, destination, evidence, risk, and hash; verify the approver's authority; reject expiry, supersession, hash mismatch, or stale resource state. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
What may an intervention change, and what may it never do? & Supply information, choose an alternative as a plan revision, or correct state with an expected version; it never approves a protected effect. \\
How do operator overrides stay accountable? & Governed lifecycle commands with expected state, operator identity, reason, and linked ticket in an append-only journal. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Where this leads next}

Human control introduces waits and resumptions; Chapter 15 makes those waits durable and gives long-running work explicit state and recovery.\par

\BookChapterCover{cover-images/chapter-15-keep-long-running-work-alive-through-failure.png}

\BookChapter{15}{Keep Long-Running Work Alive Through Failure}

\begin{bookquote}

\textbf{Evidence context:} Durable-execution systems preserve workflow progress across failures and long waits; concrete integrations and replay semantics vary by backend. \textbf{\BookCitation{R02}\BookCitation{R36}\BookCitation{R38}}

\end{bookquote}

\begin{bookcallout}[Where The Mortgage Case Stands]
The applicant waits nine days to upload the second pay statement. During the wait, workers deploy twice, policy changes, the loan officer loses a role, and one earlier request times out. The run must resume safely rather than restart from a prompt transcript.
\end{bookcallout}

\begin{bookcallout}[The Design Choice]
Represent long-running work as replay-safe workflow history plus bounded activities, durable waits, and reconciliation.
\end{bookcallout}

\section{Why long-running work needs durable state}

An agent that must wait for a document, a person, or a downstream system is no longer a request/response handler. Its state has to outlive processes, deployments, retries, and partial effects. This chapter shows how to model that work as durable execution, and why recovery is part of the design rather than an operations add-on. The platform needs a stable account of intent, state transitions, pending obligations, timers, child tasks, approvals, and external effects. Workers can then disappear and return without becoming the owners of truth.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\fontsize{7.2}{9}\selectfont
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}Y{0.1880\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{State owner} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Trust boundary} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Scale unit} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Failure containment} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Core contracts} \\
Durable workflow engine & workflow activity to external I/O & history shard · activity queue · timer & one workflow execution & WorkflowEvent · Checkpoint · WaitRegistration \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

\section{Durable state is more than chat history}

\BookDiagram{assets/diagrams/pattern-durable.pdf}{Figure 15-1 — The run outlives the process: checkpoint, die, replay, adopt}{pattern-durable}

Chat history cannot reliably encode timers, retries, child ownership, approval waits, cancellation intent, resource versions, effect receipts, or deployment compatibility. The platform rebuilds run state from event history; model and tool calls are bounded activities whose inputs and outputs are recorded by reference. This is Chapter 3's restart test made operational: a run must resume from its recorded decisions, approvals, and effect receipts even if every message and every worker is discarded.\par

The workflow reducer must be deterministic. It cannot read the clock, call a model, query a database, generate randomness, or perform network I/O during replay. Those operations become activities, signals, or recorded events. This separation makes a crashed worker replaceable.\par

\section{The durable execution contract}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required analysis} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Design conclusion} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Required analysis} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Design conclusion} \\
\endhead
Why it is difficult & Process memory is ephemeral; replay can repeat nondeterministic calls and side effects; external systems deliver duplicates or callbacks out of order; code and schemas evolve while runs are active. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Design alternatives & Background job + database; queue consumers; graph checkpointing; cloud state machines; durable execution engines such as Temporal/DBOS/Restate/Durable Functions; event-sourced custom runtime. \\
Trade-offs & Durable engines impose determinism/versioning constraints and operational cost, but remove far more bespoke recovery code. Graph checkpointers are useful for agent state but may not replace a mature cross-service workflow engine. \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Failure scenarios & Replay resends an email; worker dies after effect but before acknowledgment; old run cannot replay after code change; callback is spoofed; event arrives before subscription; retry storm exhausts provider quota. \\
Enterprise example & A mortgage-evidence agent waits days for the second pay statement and for human approval. The workflow is unloaded while idle, resumes on correlated events, rechecks authorization, and continues from the last committed effect. \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Workflow, activity, signal, query, update}

\begin{itemize}

\item \textbf{Workflow code:} deterministic control logic only. No wall-clock reads, random values, direct network calls, or model calls unless captured as replay-safe activities.

\item \textbf{Activity:} one bounded external interaction with timeout, retry class, idempotency key, heartbeat, cancellation behavior, and structured result.

\item \textbf{Checkpoint:} the replay position and state reference from which a compatible worker resumes (fields in § Records worth preserving).

\item \textbf{Signal:} authenticated external input (approval.granted, document.received, callback.completed, user.clarified) carrying a correlation key and monotonic event ID.

\item \textbf{Query:} read-only inspection of run state for users and operators; must not advance execution.

\item \textbf{Update:} authorized mutation such as budget increase, plan patch, assignee change, or safe retry, through the Run API's \lstinline[style=bookinline]!:update! (Chapter 4).

\end{itemize}

\subsection{Checkpoint policy}

Checkpoint after every irreversible or externally visible effect, before long waits, after plan revisions, and at fan-in boundaries. Do not serialize full prompt histories into every checkpoint; persist canonical state and immutable artifact references. Encrypt by tenant and apply retention by data class.\par

\section{The lifecycle of a durable run}

Every pattern in Chapters 10–14 runs inside this lifecycle.\par

\subsection{Canonical control flow}

\begin{enumerate}

\item \textbf{Admit.} Authenticate the caller; bind tenant, subject, channel, purpose, data classification, requested scope, and a client idempotency key.

\item \textbf{Snapshot.} Resolve immutable versions of the agent, workflow, prompt bundle, resolved Skill digests (Chapter 8), policy, model route, tool schemas, and knowledge index — the RunManifest (Chapter 4).

\item \textbf{Authorize.} Evaluate the requested goal and maximum capability envelope (capability envelopes, Chapter 21); allocate token, cost, time, tool, delegation, and concurrency budgets.

\item \textbf{Plan.} The model may propose a typed plan. Code validates DAG constraints, allowed capabilities, risk classes, preconditions, and budgets (plan compilation, Chapter 12; risk classes, Chapter 22).

\item \textbf{Execute.} The workflow kernel schedules replay-safe activities: routed workers (Chapter 11) and bounded ReAct activities (Chapter 10). Each effect is individually authorized using fresh context and a downscoped credential (credentials, Chapter 21).

\item \textbf{Checkpoint.} Persist state after every externally visible effect and meaningful decision; store large artifacts by immutable reference and hash.

\item \textbf{Wait or intervene.} Suspend without a worker for timers, callbacks, clarification, or approval (approval and intervention, Chapter 14). Signals carry correlation and authorization metadata.

\item \textbf{Validate and commit.} Deterministic validators test outputs and invariants. Effects pass the six gates (Chapter 2); critique may revise a draft but acceptance decides (Chapter 13).

\item \textbf{Complete.} Produce a typed result, evidence bundle, final cost/quality metrics, retention class, and terminal event.

\item \textbf{Learn safely.} Feed de-identified traces and failures into curated datasets; never let production memory or prompts self-modify without review (Chapter 25).

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Terminal correctness}

A run reaches Succeeded only through executable acceptance. A canceled run with a possibly committed write does not simply disappear; it moves through reconciliation and possibly compensation. If correctness cannot be established automatically, InterventionRequired is a valid terminal operational state.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Run state} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Reached when} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Never reached by} \\
Running / Waiting & Work is scheduled, or suspended on a timer, signal, NeedsInput request, or approval; non-terminal & Holding a worker or model session open \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
CancelRequested & Cancellation intent is recorded; non-terminal until in-flight effects reconcile & Killing workers mid-effect \\
Succeeded & Acceptance passes over authoritative state & Model text claiming completion (AP-07, Chapter 17) \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Failed & A permanent failure occurs and no effect is Unknown & A timed-out mutation, which is Unknown \\
Canceled & Cancellation intent is recorded and every effect is committed, compensated, or declined & Leaving an effect Unknown \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Expired & A deadline or wait expires under an explicit disposition & Implicit approval or silent abandonment \\
InterventionRequired & Correctness cannot be established automatically; an intervention task opens (Chapter 14) & Retrying until something works \\
\end{tabular}
\BookTableEnd

Step dispositions (Chapter 10) feed the workflow, acceptance decides (Chapters 2 and 13), and no run may close while an effect is Unknown; Chapter 22 owns effect states and compensation.\par

\section{Let events resume work without losing control}

\subsection{Event-driven resume flow}

\begin{enumerate}

\item Before reporting the run as waiting, the workflow persists a WaitRegistration: accepted sources, schemas, correlation keys, and expiry.

\item Event Ingress authenticates the event and writes the SignalInbox receipt, unique by tenant, source, and event ID.

\item The Correlator matches the event to the registration, or holds it as unmatched.

\item The signal or timer wakes the workflow, which revalidates authorization and resource versions after the wait.

\item The workflow continues from the last checkpoint, or supersedes stale approvals; Chapter 14 § Workflow integration is the approval instance.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Event architecture}

Events use the Chapter 4 CloudEvents envelope \BookCitation{R04}; producers use a transactional outbox and consumers an inbox (Chapter 22). Durable execution adds one rule: every correlation key must resolve to a wait registration or an unmatched-event holding area. A dead-letter queue is quarantine and diagnosis—not a substitute for domain compensation.\par

\section{A workable implementation}

\subsection{Who should own each decision}

\rowcolor{BookLight}

\subsection{The contracts between components}

The base Run API is in Chapter 4. Durable execution adds these operations and two semantics: \lstinline[style=bookinline]!:signal! carries event ID, correlation key, schema version, and expected state; \lstinline[style=bookinline]!:cancel! is durable intent with propagation and compensation policy, never immediate termination. History is read through Chapter 4's \lstinline[style=bookinline]!GET /v1/runs/{id}/events!, role-redacted, with content referenced rather than embedded.\par

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{API} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Purpose and contract} \\

\subsection{Records worth preserving}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}Y{0.4700\linewidth}Y{0.4700\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entity} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Key fields and invariants} \\
\endfirsthead
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Entity} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Key fields and invariants} \\
\endhead
WorkflowExecution & run ID, tenant, workflow type/build, home region/shard, state, state version, history generation, start/close times; one active execution per run generation \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
WorkflowEvent & execution ID, monotonically ordered event sequence, type, logical time, payload reference/hash, causation/correlation, producer; append-only \\
ActivityAttempt & activity ID, logical effect key, type/version, task queue, attempt, timeout set, heartbeat, result/error reference, retry disposition \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
WaitRegistration & run ID, wait type, correlation keys, accepted event schemas/sources, expiry, resume node, current status \\
SignalInbox & tenant, source, event ID, payload hash, received time, verification result, routed run; unique (tenant, source, event\_id) \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
Timer & workflow ID, timer ID, due time, purpose, cancellation state; durable and sharded by due-time bucket \\
Checkpoint & execution ID, event sequence, canonical state hash/reference, plan revision, artifact/effect cursors, code compatibility range \\
\rowcolor{BookLight}
WorkflowMigration & from/to build, applicable state/schema, deterministic transformer digest, approval, test evidence \\
\end{longtable}
\BookTableEnd

\subsection{Execution and persistence rules}

\begin{itemize}

\item Record model and external tool calls as activities whose results are persisted before workflow continuation. Replay returns the recorded result; it does not call the model or tool again.

\item Wait registration before acknowledgment; inbox before signal. Together they close the “event arrived before subscription” race.

\item For a side effect, persist intent/effect key before invocation and reconcile the receipt before an activity is considered complete (Chapter 22).

\item Use logical workflow time for decisions. Wall-clock access, random IDs, and dynamic configuration are provided as recorded events/side effects.

\item Continue-as-new or history compaction starts a new generation with a verified state snapshot when history size or event count reaches an engine-specific threshold; preserve an audit link to prior generations.

\end{itemize}

\subsection{Scalability and multi-region behavior}

\begin{itemize}

\item Shard workflows by stable run ID and timers by time bucket + shard. Activity queues are separate from workflow-task queues so slow tools cannot starve state transitions.

\item Waiting executions consume storage, not workers. Capacity planning distinguishes active workflow tasks, scheduled activities, open waits, timer cardinality, and history-write throughput.

\item Autoscale activity workers on oldest-message age, arrival rate, observed service time, provider quota, and downstream health. CPU is only a secondary signal for I/O-heavy agent tasks.

\item Use sticky execution caches for latency but require correctness without them; any compatible worker must rebuild state from history after eviction or failure.

\item Assign each run one home region with one writer per generation; Chapter 27 covers fencing, restore, reconciliation, and resume during regional recovery.

\item Separate task queues by workflow build compatibility. Keep old workers available for in-flight runs or migrate explicitly; never replay old history against untested new code. Chapter 27 makes this release policy: rollback reassigns new work only.

\item Rate-limit external signals per tenant/source and coalesce superseding updates where domain semantics allow. Preserve individual inbox receipts even when only the latest value wakes the workflow.

\end{itemize}

\section{Durable execution at a glance}

Use for work with waits, multiple systems, protected effects, independent failures, or duration beyond one request. & Avoid a workflow engine for a single short idempotent read with no recovery or coordination requirement. \\

\item workflow build

\section{What the evidence lets us conclude}

\needspace{5\baselineskip}
\BookTableStart
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}Y{0.3133\linewidth}@{}}
\rowcolor{BookNavy}
\color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Claim or correction} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Grounded statement} & \color{white}\bfseries \textbf{Source IDs} \\
